First hypothesis (recorded, then disproved): a genuine, pre-existing, volume-sensitive concurrency issue in the test harness's script-check worker-thread lifecycle. **Disproved directly**: the incremental binary had been rebuilt dozens of times in rapid succession during this same session's own mutation-testing cycles (edit a source file, rebuild, run, restore the file via `cp`, rebuild again -- repeated for every mutant of every fix, across F-140 through this entry). A `make clean` followed by one full rebuild, then 8 further full runs of `acceptancebit_tests` against that clean binary, produced 8/8 clean -- zero failures, no hang. The incremental Makefile's own dependency tracking most likely missed a rebuild boundary somewhere across that many rapid edit/restore/rebuild cycles, linking a stale `.o` against fresher ones -- a classic ODR/ABI-mismatch class of bug that manifests as random, unrelated-looking corruption, exactly what was observed. **Lesson for this project's own mutation-testing discipline**: after a long session of many manual mutate-rebuild-revert cycles on the same files, do a full clean rebuild before trusting a flakiness signal as a real finding -- an incremental build carrying that much edit/restore churn is not a reliable basis for a concurrency diagnosis. Verified the fix holds under load, not just by inspection: the live file's mtime (`1790078370`) was read before and after a full clean-rebuilt suite run and was byte-identical both times. **The stray file itself was left untouched** -- per this project's own F-127 precedent (owner confirms before deleting anything in a live personal resource outside the repo), it was flagged to the owner directly rather than removed. **HIGH (test-quality, same root cause)**: F-146's own claim that the "ignore the serveability lookup's return value" mutant died via `validate_misses_a_withheld_hash` was **false** -- re-running that exact mutant on a datadir the live-node bug had been polluting for weeks found it actually SURVIVED; the original "kill" was a coincidence of stale garbage bytes at the hand-built `FlatFilePos(0, 100)` test position (never actually written) happening to make `ReadBodyRecordCount` reject it as a corrupt count, an accident of the same contamination as the file above, not the serveability check the test claimed to prove. **Fixed** by rewriting `validate_misses_a_withheld_hash` to back the withheld entry with a REAL `FindBodyPos`/`WriteBodyRecord`-written record, so the property under test can no longer be coincidentally protected by an unwritten position. **Re-verifying this mutant on the corrected, isolated fixture found something more interesting than a plain confirmation**: it still survives -- but now for a benign, structural reason rather than a hidden gap. The MEDIUM fix below means `LookupServeableBodyPositionByHash` never writes to the caller's `posOut` on ANY failure path (confirmed: `FlatFilePos`'s default constructor is `nFile(-1), nPos(0)`, `flatfile.h`), so a caller that ignores its boolean return still gets a default/untouched `posOut`, and `ReadBodyRecordCount`'s own `OpenBodyFile` fails to open file `-1` regardless of whether the underlying hash was unknown or genuinely withheld -- both paths now reach MISS through the same null-position safety net, making "ignoring this function's return value" harmless by construction at this call site, not merely untested. Confirmed the REAL property (serveability actually enforced, not just "a caller who checks return values is fine") is caught correctly by a mutant one layer down -- deleting the `|| !fServeable` half of `LookupServeableBodyPositionByHash`'s own condition -- which dies four ways: both `bodystore_tests.cpp` serveability cases, and both `bodyrange_tests.cpp` cases that route through it. **MEDIUM**: `LookupServeableBodyPositionByHash` leaked a withheld block's real position into the caller's `posOut` on its false-return path -- `LookupBodyPositionByHash` writes `posOut` the instant an entry is FOUND, before the wrapper's own `fServeable` check runs, so the original direct pass-through left a real, valid position sitting in `posOut` even when returning false. TDD: new `bodystore_tests.cpp` case (`lookup_serveable_body_position_by_hash_leaves_posout_untouched_on_a_withheld_miss`) confirmed RED before the fix (`check posOut.IsNull() has failed`). **Fixed** by resolving into a local `FlatFilePos tmpPos` first, assigning to the caller's `posOut` only on the genuine success path. **LOW**: `cbodyrange_round_trips_preserving_order_and_content`'s data-carrying case never asserted `hashBlock`/`nStartIndex`, only the empty-`vBodies` case did -- a `SERIALIZE_METHODS` field-order swap between `hashBlock` and `vBodies` could pass unnoticed; fixed, and `nStartIndex` changed from `0` to `4` so the new assertion is meaningful. **LOW**: `vtx_index_from_body_index_matches_a_real_accepted_block` indexed `block.vtx[vtxIndex]` with no bounds check -- an off-by-one mutant of `VtxIndexFromBodyIndex` would index out of bounds and crash via UB rather than fail a clean assertion; fixed with `BOOST_REQUIRE_LT(vtxIndex, block.vtx.size())`. **LOW, missing coverage**: no test exercised `ReadBodyRecordCount` failing on a serveable-but-corrupt record (must be MISS, not BAN) -- added `validate_misses_a_serveable_hash_with_a_corrupt_record` (writes an implausible `CompactSize` count directly via `CAutoFile`/`WriteCompactSize`). **LOW, missing coverage**: the coinbase-only (empty body record) BAN-by-construction case was never tested or documented as intentional -- added `validate_bans_any_request_on_a_coinbase_only_record` (`WriteBodyRecord(pos, {})`). **LOW, deferred to 2.2.3, not fixed here**: a null `CTransactionRef` in `CBodyRange::vBodies` is reachable once a serving handler exists and a mid-range `ReadBodyAt` fails -- recorded as a requirement for 2.2.3's own implementation plan (truncate the response at the last good body on any read failure, never leave a hole), not actionable against code that doesn't exist yet. Full mutation re-verification on the corrected, isolated fixture after all fixes (genuine `make clean` + full rebuild, this session's own F-144/F-145 lesson applied again): the 5 mutants F-146 already tried (wrong `nCount` sentinel, overflow check deleted, off-by-one on out-of-range-start -- now caught two ways, off-by-one on `VtxIndexFromBodyIndex` -- now caught seventeen ways via the new bounds check) still die, plus the two new mutants above (the call-site return-value mutant, now correctly understood as structurally inert rather than "fixed"; and the real serveability-check mutant one layer down, which dies four ways). 3 new tests. Full suite: 519 cases (`!miner_tests`) clean on a genuine clean rebuild, up from 516; `miner_tests` alone shows only the one known pre-existing `CreateNewBlock_validity` failure, unchanged | Fable adversarial review of commit 81dd414b4, 2026-09-22; live-datadir claim verified directly (`stat`, `ps`) not taken on the reviewer's word; mutation-tested (7 mutants total across this and F-146, all die or are shown structurally inert for a stated reason) | **fixed 2026-09-22** |
| F-148 | **A second Fable review, of F-147's own fix (commit a5c3aeb2b), found F-147's central "structurally inert" safety claim doesn't generalize past its one current caller, plus a genuine sibling-sweep gap and two more test-quality holes -- 1 MEDIUM, 1 MEDIUM (systemic), 2 LOW, all fixed** | **MEDIUM**: F-147's own claim that ignoring `LookupServeableBodyPositionByHash`'s return value is "harmless by construction" is true only because `bodyrange.cpp`'s one caller always passes a FRESH `FlatFilePos` -- the function's real contract was "leaves `posOut` untouched on failure", not "posOut is null on failure", and those are only equivalent for a caller who never reuses the variable. Verified directly by simulating the obvious next caller: with the ignore-return mutant applied, pre-loading `posOut` with a real position before calling for an unknown OR a withheld hash returns `OK` instead of `MISS` -- exactly the shape a per-connection serving loop (2.2.3, not yet built) that reuses one `FlatFilePos` across requests would hit, carrying a PRIOR successful lookup's real position into a later miss. **Fixed** by making the null guarantee the function's own unconditional contract: `LookupServeableBodyPositionByHash` now calls `posOut.SetNull()` (`FlatFilePos::SetNull`, `flatfile.h`) on every failure path, not merely refraining from writing it. TDD: replaced the one existing test that only proved this against a freshly-default-constructed `posOut` (passes trivially, since default-constructed is already null) with two that deliberately pre-load `posOut` with `FlatFilePos(3, 77)` before calling, for both the unknown-hash and withheld-hash cases -- both confirmed RED against the pre-fix code (`check posOut.IsNull() has failed`), both GREEN after, and reverting the fix (deleting the `SetNull()` call) is caught by both. **MEDIUM, systemic**: the live-datadir bug class F-127 fixed once and F-147 fixed a second time (in two different files, each with its own one-off fixture) is now fixed at the root: `BasicTestingSetup` (`test/test_raptoreum.cpp`) already computes a private, destructor-cleaned `m_path_root` for every instance but never pointed `-datadir` at it -- confirmed directly by reading `TestingSetup`'s own constructor, which does exactly `SetDataDir("tempdir"); ClearDatadirCache();` immediately after its `BasicTestingSetup` base runs, meaning only the heavier fixture ever got real isolation. **Fixed** by moving those same two calls into `BasicTestingSetup`'s own constructor -- harmless where `TestingSetup` now repeats them (idempotent: recreates the same directory, re-sets the same arg). This closes the whole bug class by construction rather than by convention: a third file can no longer reintroduce it just by inheriting `BasicTestingSetup` directly. The now-fully-redundant `BodyRangeTestingSetup` (F-147's own one-off fixture, added and removed in the same review chain) was deleted, reverting `bodyrange_tests.cpp` to `BasicTestingSetup` directly; `bodystore_tests.cpp`'s `BodyStoreTestingSetup` (F-127's, which also constructs a real `pblocktree` and so still earns its own struct) had its now-redundant `SetDataDir`/`ClearDatadirCache` calls removed, comments in both files corrected to describe the fix's actual location. Sibling sweep performed independently (not merely trusting the reviewer's own sweep): grepped every `src/test/*.cpp` file for `BasicTestingSetup` combined with any real disk I/O (body-store calls, `CDBWrapper`, raw `fopen`/`ofstream`) -- confirmed the only three files performing real body-store I/O are `bodystore_tests.cpp`, `bodyrange_tests.cpp` (both already covered) and `acceptancebit_tests.cpp` (already on `TestChain100Setup`, which inherits `TestingSetup`'s own isolation, never was at risk). **LOW**: the comment F-147 added claiming its new `hashBlock`/`nStartIndex` round-trip assertions would catch "a `SERIALIZE_METHODS` field-order swap" was itself wrong -- `SERIALIZE_METHODS` generates ONE ordered list both the writer and reader walk identically, so a pure reorder changes both sides at once and a round-trip test can never observe it (verified directly: reordering `CBodyRange`'s fields and rebuilding still passes every round-trip test in the file). **Fixed** by correcting the comment and adding what actually pins a wire format: a golden-hex test (`cgetbodyrange_wire_format_is_pinned`, `CGetBodyRange` chosen over `CBodyRange` to avoid coupling this test to `TransactionCompression`'s own encoding, a separate concern) -- mutation-verified to catch the exact field-order swap the old comment claimed the round-trip test caught. **LOW**: the overflow-BAN boundary (`nStartIndex + nCount == UINT32_MAX`, legal and non-overflowing) was never tested -- only genuine overflows were -- so the off-by-one mutant `>` to `>=` on that check survived every test in the file. **Fixed** with `validate_does_not_ban_the_exact_non_overflowing_boundary`, mutation-verified to catch it. Full mutation re-verification: both new production fixes (the `SetNull()` contract, the base-fixture datadir isolation) and both new test-quality fixes (golden-hex, boundary) confirmed to catch their own named mutant, individually, on a genuine `make clean` + full rebuild. 4 net new tests (2 replacing 1). | F-149 | **2.2.3a built: the GETBODYRANGE serving handler, the first off-`cs_main` block-serving path anywhere in this tree, plus its own pure, TDD-tested response-building logic -- one real mutation-testing gap found and closed in the author's own first draft (a DoS-class bug, not a coverage cosmetic)** | New `BuildBodyRangeResponse(request, pos, nByteCeiling, respOut)` (`bodyrange.h`/`.cpp`, extending 2.2.2's existing pure-function convention): given a position `ValidateGetBodyRange` already classified `OK`, reads bodies one at a time via `ReadBodyAt`, stopping at whichever comes first -- `nCount` bodies read; the next body would push the response's own running `SER_NETWORK`/`PROTOCOL_VERSION` serialized size past `nByteCeiling` (except the FIRST body is always force-included regardless of its own size, so a real miss and "ceiling smaller than any transaction" never look identical on the wire); or a `ReadBodyAt` call fails (the record legitimately has fewer bodies than requested past `nStartIndex` -- `ValidateGetBodyRange`'s own `OK` only guarantees `nStartIndex` itself is in range, never `nStartIndex + nCount`). New dispatch: `net_processing.cpp`'s `ProcessMessage` gains a `NetMsgType::GETBODYRANGE` arm and a `SendBodyRange` helper (matching `SendBlockTransactions`'s own precedent, the closest existing request-with-indices-serve-real-data handler) -- `ValidateGetBodyRange`/`BuildBodyRangeResponse` resolve entirely through 2.2.1's own body-store index/files (`cs_bodyIndex` only), and `cs_main` is taken ONLY for the brief, purely in-memory `Misbehaving()` call on the `BAN` path (`Misbehaving` is itself `EXCLUSIVE_LOCKS_REQUIRED(cs_main)`, confirmed directly, since it reads/writes per-peer `CNodeState` -- a fast critical section, not the I/O-under-`cs_main` class of problem B4 names, which is exactly what `ProcessGetBlockData`, this tree's only prior block-serving path, does today and what this handler was built not to repeat). New `DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_BYTES` (1 MiB) constant with two `static_assert`s tying it, and separately `MAX_STANDARD_TX_SIZE`, to `MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH` with a 4096-byte margin -- proving a chunked response and a forced-through oversized first body can BOTH never overflow one P2P message; grounded in a real consensus fact, not merely a policy one, verified directly: `ContextualCheckTransaction` (`validation.cpp:482`) rejects any transaction over `MAX_STANDARD_TX_SIZE` with `bad-txns-oversize` once DIP0001 is active, so no transaction that could ever reach a real body record exceeds it. **Explicit scope boundary, matching this phase's own established pattern of partial, reviewed increments**: NO announcer ring (misbehaviour tracking for who owes an answer -- F-143's own design, not yet built, belongs with 2.2.4's fetching side or its own sub-phase); NO per-connection records-and-bytes budget or `-maxuploadtarget` integration (F-143's own explicitly-deferred backpressure layer -- today's byte-ceiling chunking bounds a SINGLE response's cost, not the aggregate cost of many requests from one peer); NO runtime `-maxbodyrangebytes` config knob (a fixed, generously-margined compiled default is used instead -- F-146 assigned this knob to 2.2.3, but it is not load-bearing for correctness/safety and is left to whichever later increment builds the full budget system it would naturally belong to); NO hash-mismatch/over-delivery/unsolicited-late-response misbehaviour rules (these check an INCOMING `BODYRANGE` response, i.e. the fetching/client side, 2.2.4's job, not this serving handler). The announcer ring's own still-open dependency from F-143 (its depth `D` "coupled to... this project's still-unfixed minimum body-retention floor") was resolved by research, not left open: `MIN_BLOCKS_TO_KEEP` (`validation.h`, 288) already governs exactly this question for pruning (`FindFilesToPrune`'s `nLastBlockWeCanPrune = Tip()->nHeight - MIN_BLOCKS_TO_KEEP`, `validation.cpp:5276`, confirmed directly) -- the exact same constant, under its `NODE_NETWORK_LIMITED_MIN_BLOCKS` alias, is already reused in `net_processing.cpp:1749` for an analogous "how far back can a peer be trusted to still have data" bound, with a `+2` race-buffer margin -- so whichever increment builds the ring should reuse it rather than invent a new number; recorded here rather than re-opened as unresolved, since a later builder needs the answer, not the question. TDD throughout: `BuildBodyRangeResponse` written test-first (confirmed RED via a real compile failure -- the function didn't exist), 6 new `bodyrange_tests.cpp` cases (`nCount`-respecting, truncate-on-record-end, force-include-the-first-body-over-a-tiny-ceiling, stop-at-the-ceiling-once-one-body-is-held, echo hashBlock/nStartIndex). Mutation-tested, 5 mutants: deleting the forced-first-body exception, deleting the ceiling check entirely (2 tests independently catch it), and turning the `ReadBodyAt`-failure `break` into `continue` -- died on 4/5 instantly; the 5th (`break`→`continue`) SURVIVED every content-based assertion, since once reads start failing every later index also fails and the final `vBodies` comes out identical either way -- the only observable difference is how many doomed `ReadBodyAt` calls get made, and with `nCount` bounded only by `ValidateGetBodyRange`'s own overflow check (up to `UINT32_MAX - nStartIndex`), a peer could otherwise force billions of wasted file-open attempts against a record with one real body -- a genuine DoS surface in the author's own first draft, not a hypothetical one: reproduced directly, the `continue` mutant provably hung past 30 seconds under a 5-second timing assertion built for exactly this. New test added (`build_response_does_not_hammer_reads_past_the_records_real_end`, timing-bounded, a large margin given the magnitude gap involved -- microseconds correct, many minutes-to-hours buggy) closes it; the correct (`break`) implementation was already shipped, so this was a coverage gap catching a real property of already-correct code, not a late-discovered production bug. Verified via a genuine clean rebuild (this session's own recurring lesson about incremental-build staleness). Full suite: 528 cases (`!miner_tests`) clean, up from 522; `miner_tests` alone unchanged (the one known pre-existing failure). `net_processing.cpp`'s own dispatch arm has no unit coverage, matching this tree's already-established, accepted convention for message-processing glue (`SENDCMPCT` is the same way, per F-136's own finding) -- every byte of actual response-building LOGIC lives in the fully-tested `bodyrange.cpp` instead, deliberately, so the untested surface is glue only | `source` (Misbehaving's own `EXCLUSIVE_LOCKS_REQUIRED` annotation, `PushMessage`'s lack of one, `ContextualCheckTransaction`'s real DIP0001-gated consensus check, `MIN_BLOCKS_TO_KEEP`'s existing pruning/peer-trust reuse -- all confirmed directly, not assumed from the spec's own prose), mutation-tested (5 mutants, one caught only by a timing-bounded reproduction of a genuine DoS hang, not a mutation-testing formality) | **built 2026-09-22** |
| F-150 | **Fable review of F-149 (commit 43d1cf3d8) found a genuine, measured 12-second-per-request DoS bug in the author's own shipped code -- not deferred scope, a bug -- plus a real but defensible scope gap, and two test-quality holes. All fixed; the scope gap recorded as a hard deployment gate, not silently accepted** | **HIGH, confirmed by direct source reading before accepting**: `ReadBodyAt` (`bodystore.h`/`.cpp`, built in an earlier phase, long before 2.2.3) opens the record and re-reads its ENTIRE offset table on every single call -- `BuildBodyRangeResponse` calling it once per body in a range request made this an O(`nCount` x the record's own real body count) cost, not the O(bodies actually read) the design assumed. The reviewer measured ~12.3s of real CPU on `ThreadMessageHandler` (`net.cpp:3015`, the SAME thread `ProcessMessage` also dispatches LLMQ DKG/ChainLock/sig-share messages on, `net_processing.cpp`) for ONE `GETBODYRANGE` request against a 100,000-body record; at `COMMITMENT_BUDGET_MAX_INPUTS` (700,000) the same request would be ~90s. **Fixed** with a new store primitive, `ReadBodyRange(pos, nStartIndex, nMaxCount, nByteCeiling, bodiesOut)` (`bodystore.h`/`.cpp`) -- opens the file and reads the offset table exactly ONCE, seeks once to `nStartIndex`, then reads forward sequentially (each deserialize already advances the file position, so no further seeking is needed), giving O(record size) total regardless of how many bodies are requested. `BuildBodyRangeResponse` is now a thin wrapper delegating to it -- same TDD'd black-box contract (`nMaxCount`/ceiling/force-include-the-first-body/truncate-on-end), same 6 pre-existing tests still green unchanged, since only the internal cost profile moved. TDD: `ReadBodyRange` written test-first, 7 new `bodystore_tests.cpp` cases including a genuine reproduction at meaningful scale (50,000 fixed-size bodies -- deliberately NOT the existing `MakeBodies` helper, which pads by index and would make a 50,000th body 50,000 bytes on its own, exceeding what `FindBodyPos` correctly refuses) asserting completion well under a real 3-second bound; mutation-verified directly by reintroducing the exact quadratic shape (calling `ReadBodyAt` in a loop) as `ReadBodyRange`'s own body -- caught cleanly (test failed the 3-second bound, did not hang) at this record size, unlike F-149's own now-superseded timing test (see LOW below). 4 more mutants (forced-first-body deleted, ceiling check deleted -- 2 tests independently catch it, the initial seek skipped) all die. As a direct side effect, this also **fixes F-149's own MEDIUM-3-class issue** the reviewer separately flagged: the honest "asked past the record's real end" case no longer risks touching any error/logging path at all -- `ReadBodyRange`'s own loop bound is clamped to the offset table's real size before the read loop even starts, so an out-of-range index is never attempted, not merely caught after failing. **MEDIUM, real but defensible, NOT silently accepted**: confirmed directly (grepped the whole file) that `net_processing.cpp` has no per-message-type rate limit anywhere -- `nReceiveFloodSize` only bounds queued UNPROCESSED bytes (~125,000 queued 40-byte requests at the 5 MB default), `fPauseSend`/`DEFAULT_MAXSENDBUFFER` only throttles a peer that stops reading its own socket, and `-maxuploadtarget` is consulted only for historical `ProcessGetBlockData` serving and `MEMPOOL`, never for `BODYRANGE` bytes -- unlike `GETBLOCKTXN`'s own `MAX_BLOCKTXN_DEPTH`, built specifically (its own comment) to stop "lots of getblocktxn requests to trigger expensive disk reads", `GETBODYRANGE` has no analogue. With the HIGH fixed, per-request cost now scales with bytes actually served (≈ today's already-accepted class of unbudgeted serving cost, per file open), not a hidden multiplier -- the reviewer's own assessment: "defensible as a partial increment on a branch" PROVIDED this is not deployed exposed before the real per-connection budget (F-143's own already-recorded, still-deferred scope item) lands. **Recorded as a hard deployment gate, not just a scope note**: `net_processing.cpp`'s own `DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_BYTES` doc comment now states explicitly that 2.2.3a must not run on a live/exposed network until the budget layer exists; build-plan.md's 2.2 row carries the same gate. **LOW**: F-149's own `build_response_does_not_hammer_reads_past_the_records_real_end` test (`nCount = UINT32_MAX`) would have HUNG rather than failed cleanly against a reintroduced quadratic bug at that unbounded scale -- reproduced directly (killed at a 60s external timeout, zero Boost output, matching a job that looks stuck rather than one that looks red). Not re-armed at that scale; comment corrected to record that the real, properly-bounded regression guard now lives in `bodystore_tests.cpp`, and this test's own remaining, narrower value (the wrapper stays fast and correct at the extreme legal `nCount`) is stated honestly rather than left implying it's still the main guard. **LOW**: the ceiling-check's exact boundary (`nRunningBytes + nTxBytes == nByteCeiling`, landing exactly on it) was untested -- an off-by-one mutant (`>` to `>=`) survived every test in the file. **Fixed** with `read_body_range_includes_a_body_that_lands_exactly_on_the_ceiling`, mutation-verified. **LOW, doc accuracy**: a stale `docs/bodyrange.h` path in a `net_processing.cpp` comment corrected to `src/bodyrange.h`; F-149's own `net_processing.cpp:1749` citation for the `NODE_NETWORK_LIMITED_MIN_BLOCKS` reuse had already drifted to ~1770-1775 by the time of this review (this project's own accepted unkeyed-citation backlog, per `check-anchors.py`'s own header comment -- not re-edited in F-149's already-committed text, noted here instead). | F-151 | **A third, targeted Fable review of 2.2.3a (commit `4bc4b9313`) converges: the performance fix holds under direct measurement, one further real-but-smaller performance gap found and fixed, the rate-limit gap upgraded from a documented risk to an enforced one, 3 more LOW items closed. No HIGH this round -- this piece is now considered settled** | **Convergence context**: this was explicitly the second review round on 2.2.3a (after F-150), requested narrower/targeted rather than full-scope, matching this project's own two-round pattern for 2.2.1/2.2.2. It found no correctness bug and no HIGH -- the strongest possible outcome short of "nothing at all," and the signal this project uses to stop iterating on a piece. **MEDIUM**: `ReadBodyRange`'s F-150 fix eliminated the O(bodies read × record size) cost but left a smaller one: it still read ALL `count` offsets from the shared `ReadBodyRecordHeader` helper regardless of `nStartIndex`/`nMaxCount`, so even a single-body request paid the full record's own header cost -- independently measured at ~5.2ms against a real 700,000-body (`COMMITMENT_BUDGET_MAX_INPUTS`) record, the SAME cost for `nStartIndex=0`, `350,000`, or `699,999`. At ~200 req/s (≈8 KB/s inbound, well within a single handshaked peer's easy reach) this alone pins `ThreadMessageHandler`. **Fixed**: `ReadBodyRange` no longer calls the shared helper -- it reads the `CompactSize` count directly, then (for `nStartIndex > 0`) seeks straight to the ONE offset-table entry it actually needs (`offsets[nStartIndex-1]`, at `(nStartIndex-1) * BODY_OFFSET_WIDTH` bytes into the table) and reads just that value, then seeks past the table's remaining entries (an arithmetic skip, never read) plus that one offset to land exactly at the target body -- O(1) header cost instead of O(record size). Calibrated, not guessed: reused the existing 300,000-body performance fixture (scaled up from F-150's 50,000, which the reviewer found gave an unreliable ~1.3x gap, too marginal for a robust regression test) and measured directly on the build machine -- correct code: ~32µs for a single-body request; the pre-fix (F-150) shape reintroduced as a mutant: ~2.5ms at the same scale, a ~78x gap -- the new test's 1ms bound sits with large margin on both sides (~30x above correct, ~2.5x below buggy), mutation-verified to actually catch the regression (confirmed empirically: caught cleanly, not via a hang). Two further mutants of the new seek arithmetic (an off-by-one in the remaining-table-bytes skip; discarding the read offset value instead of using it) both die -- the first across 11 assertions in both `bodystore_tests.cpp` and `bodyrange_tests.cpp`, the second across 5, confirming the existing content-based tests (not just the new timing one) exercise the new code path thoroughly. **MEDIUM, upgraded from documented to enforced**: the reviewer's own honest assessment was that F-150's doc-comment-only gate ("must not run on a live/exposed network") has no failure mode that makes itself visible if ignored, and this project's own established convention for an unfinished feature is executable, not prose (`g_commitmentBudgetActive`, `-perfwithhold*` -- both checked directly before treating them as precedent). **Fixed**: new `-servebodyrange` arg (`init.cpp`, `ArgsManager::DEBUG_ONLY`/`OptionsCategory::DEBUG_TEST`, matching the `-perfwithhold*`/`-perfskipsigs` registration convention exactly), default `false`; the `GETBODYRANGE` dispatch arm (`net_processing.cpp`) now checks it FIRST and silently declines (`return true`, no `Misbehaving`) when unset, matching this codebase's own confirmed-safe "ignore unknown commands for extensibility" precedent (F-31) rather than banning a peer for sending a real, spec-valid message this node has simply chosen not to serve yet. `net_processing.cpp`'s own dispatch-glue convention (no unit coverage, matching `SENDCMPCT`) means this specific gate has no dedicated test, same as the rest of the arm -- verified by direct reading instead. **LOW**: the truncation contract ("a corrupt body mid-range is treated exactly like reaching the record's real end, never logged") holds only for corruption that actually throws -- a zero-filled body decodes without error as a valid-looking empty transaction and desyncs the stream, returning garbage bodies rather than truncating. Confirmed real by direct reproduction (zero-filling a body and reading the range back). **Not fixed** -- explicitly recorded as an accepted, deferred gap: this store has no on-disk-corruption-inducing code path today (confirmed: the sole writer is `WriteBodyRecord` via `SaveBodyToDisk`, records are never rewritten or pruned, and the hash-index entry is recorded only after the write completes and the file is closed, `validation.cpp`, so a torn read via the index is structurally impossible), and the real defence against this class of thing either way is the requester-side per-body hash check (§5.2, 2.2.4's own job, not this handler's). **LOW**: `nStartIndex >= count` returning `true` silently (not logged, not an error) is a real, documented contract, but nothing exercised it directly -- a `return true -> return false` mutant on that exact branch survived both suites (the case is unreachable from the wire today, since `ValidateGetBodyRange`'s own `BAN` tier catches it first, but the header makes an unconditional claim about `ReadBodyRange`'s own behaviour as a standalone function). **Fixed** with a direct one-line test. **LOW, doc accuracy**: a stale "HIGH-2" severity label in a `net_processing.cpp` comment (the finding was reclassified to MEDIUM before F-150 was even written up) corrected in place. Full suite: 535 cases (`!miner_tests`) clean on a genuine clean rebuild (test count unchanged from F-150 -- this round augmented two existing tests rather than adding new cases, plus one new one-line test); `miner_tests` alone unchanged (the one known pre-existing failure) | Third Fable adversarial review, targeted/narrower scope, of commit 4bc4b9313, 2026-09-22; the O(record size) header-cost claim independently reproduced with real measurements on the build machine (not merely trusted from the reviewer's own numbers, which were taken at a different N); the executable-gate judgment call independently weighed against this codebase's own two real precedents before accepting; mutation-tested (3 new mutants -- 1 timing-based, 2 correctness-based -- all die) | **fixed 2026-09-23, converged -- no further review round scheduled** |
| F-152 | **2.2.3b built: the announcer ring, F-143's own 5-round-reviewed design implemented for real -- every insertion point independently re-verified against current source before coding, not assumed from the spec's own possibly-stale prose, and one of the spec's own confirmed live gaps (only a HEADERS batch's trailing header gets recorded) reproduced directly before being closed** | New `CAnnouncerRing` (`announcerring.h`/`.cpp`, matching `bodyrange.h`'s own established split between pure, fully-tested logic and untested `net_processing.cpp` glue): `Record(height, hash, currentTipHeight, depth)` records a peer's announcement, evicting entries more than `depth` blocks behind `currentTipHeight` first (memory hygiene only, not the correctness boundary) and refusing to insert an already-stale entry; `WasAnnounced(hash, currentTipHeight, depth)` re-derives freshness itself at query time rather than trusting that `Record`'s own eviction ran recently -- a caller gets a correct answer regardless of insertion/query ordering, matching this session's own repeated lesson (F-148) that a safety property should be the function's own unconditional contract, not an accident of caller behaviour. Keyed by hash (`std::unordered_map<uint256, int, StaticSaltedHasher>`), not by height alone -- a peer can legitimately announce two different blocks at the same height (competing forks) before either is known to win, and a height-keyed design would lose one to the other. TDD throughout, 8 new `announcerring_tests.cpp` cases; 4 mutants (the stale-entry insertion guard deleted, the eviction loop deleted, `WasAnnounced` ignoring freshness entirely, an off-by-one on its exact boundary) all die. **Wiring** (`net_processing.cpp`, `CNodeState` gains an `announcerRing` member): every insertion point was independently re-verified against CURRENT source before coding, given F-143's own review found real, live escape hatches at every round when insertion points were merely assumed. Confirmed directly: `UpdateBlockAvailability`/`ProcessBlockAvailability` (`net_processing.cpp`) are genuinely distinct resolution branches, not one calling the other "for free" -- both now call a new shared `RecordAnnouncer(nodeid, pindex)` helper inside their own `if (pindex && pindex->nChainWork > 0)` branch, unconditionally (not gated on "did this become the new best-known block", since the ring's job is "did this peer announce THIS block", not "what is their current best tip"). Confirmed INV already routes through `UpdateBlockAvailability` with no separate path (so needs no insertion of its own) and that the unconnecting-headers deferred-resolution path (`headers.back().GetHash()` recorded unresolved via `UpdateBlockAvailability`'s own `hashLastUnknownBlock` branch) is genuinely resolved LATER, and ONLY, by `ProcessBlockAvailability`'s own separate branch -- both spec claims verified against source, not merely trusted from F-143's own prose. **Reproduced directly, not assumed, before fixing**: read `ChainstateManager::ProcessNewBlockHeaders` (`validation.cpp`) and confirmed its `pindexLast` out-param is populated incrementally, per header, even on a call that ultimately returns `false` -- and confirmed the current `ProcessHeadersMessage` (`net_processing.cpp`) genuinely only calls `UpdateBlockAvailability` with the batch's own TRAILING header hash (`pindexLast->GetBlockHash()`), meaning every other header in an accepted batch, and the entire accepted prefix of a batch that later hits an invalid header, gets zero announcer-ring record today -- this is a live gap in the current tree, not a historical one already fixed elsewhere. **Fixed** with a new `RecordAnnouncedHeaderRange(nodeid, headers, pindexLast)` helper: walks `headers[0..]`, resolving and recording each one via `RecordAnnouncer` until reaching whichever header matches `pindexLast`'s own hash (a no-op if `pindexLast` is null, i.e. the very first header was itself invalid) -- called from BOTH `ProcessHeadersMessage`'s success path (immediately after the existing trailing `UpdateBlockAvailability` call, which remains responsible for `pindexBestKnownBlock`/`hashLastUnknownBlock` bookkeeping unrelated to the ring) and its specific partial-failure sub-path (before the early `return false`, still under the `cs_main` that branch already holds). Recording the same hash more than once across these call sites (e.g. `pindexLast`'s own hash via both the trailing `UpdateBlockAvailability`-triggered `RecordAnnouncer` call and the loop's own last iteration) is harmless -- `CAnnouncerRing::Record` is idempotent for a repeat hash at the same height, confirmed by its own unit tests. `ANNOUNCER_RING_DEPTH` resolves F-143's own explicitly-left-open dependency ("coupled to, never exceeding, this project's still-unfixed minimum body-retention floor") by reusing `NODE_NETWORK_LIMITED_MIN_BLOCKS` (=`MIN_BLOCKS_TO_KEEP`=288, F-149/F-150's own prior research) with the same `+2` race-buffer margin already used for the identical purpose a few hundred lines below in the same file, rather than inventing a third name for the same number. **Explicit scope boundary**: this is the record-and-query mechanism only -- consuming it (actually deciding misbehaviour when a body fetch later fails against a peer this ring says announced the block) is 2.2.4's job, since that is where the fetching/retry logic itself lives; nothing calls `WasAnnounced` yet. New source files (`announcerring.h`/`.cpp`, `test/announcerring_tests.cpp`) required regenerating `Makefile.in`/`Makefile` via `automake --add-missing` + `./config.status`, matching 2.2.2's own established precedent (both confirmed gitignored via `git ls-files`/`git check-ignore` before regenerating). Verified via a genuine clean rebuild. Full suite: 543 cases (`!miner_tests`) clean, up from 535 (8 new); `miner_tests` alone unchanged (the one known pre-existing failure). `net_processing.cpp`'s own three call-site wiring has no unit coverage, matching this tree's already-established, accepted convention for message-processing glue -- every byte of the ring's own record/evict/query logic lives in the fully-tested `announcerring.cpp` instead | `source` (every insertion point re-verified directly against current `net_processing.cpp`/`validation.cpp`, not taken from F-143's own review prose, which could have drifted; the "only trailing header recorded" gap specifically reproduced by reading the live code before writing a fix for it), mutation-tested (4 mutants, all die) | **built 2026-09-23** |
| F-153 | **Fable review of F-152 (commit 69d5607aa) found a genuine HIGH: the ring's own "height-bounded, not count-bounded" design conflated the CONNECTED chain tip with the header sync frontier, making it unbounded and quadratic during any long catch-up -- measured directly at 692s for 200,000 calls before the fix, 81ms after. All fixed** | **HIGH, reproduced independently before fixing, not taken on the reviewer's own numbers alone**: `Record`'s eviction only removed entries with `height < currentTipHeight - depth` -- but `currentTipHeight` (`::ChainActive().Height()`) is the CONNECTED chain's own tip, which during headers-first sync lags far behind the headers actually being accepted (and so recorded, via `RecordAnnouncedHeaderRange`) for a peer racing ahead. Every one of those far-ahead entries sits "above the tip" and so was never evicted, and `Record`'s own eviction scan is O(ring size) on every call -- together, unbounded growth times an O(n) scan is O(n²). Confirmed the mechanism directly (re-read `Record`'s own eviction condition before accepting the reviewer's framing), then independently reproduced the reviewer's own scale via a temporary scratch probe (not merely trusted): 692.4s for 200,000 `Record` calls against real mainnet-scale heights (905,760+) before the fix. **Fixed**: a new `EffectiveHeight(currentTipHeight)` = `max(currentTipHeight, m_maxRecordedHeight)` -- the ring now tracks the highest height it has ever recorded and bounds the cutoff against WHICHEVER is higher, that or the caller-supplied tip, so it stays a bounded window around wherever the peer's own header stream has actually reached, correctly degrading back to plain `currentTipHeight` once real sync catches up (the steady-state, post-IBD case every original test already covered, still green unchanged). Re-verified the SAME scratch reproduction against the fix: 20ms/41ms/81ms for 50,000/100,000/200,000 -- linear, not quadratic, an ~8,500x improvement at the largest size, size correctly bounded to 291 (`depth+1`) regardless of how many were recorded. `WasAnnounced` updated to the same `EffectiveHeight` basis for consistency (an entry `Record` would keep is now exactly one `WasAnnounced` says is fresh, and vice versa -- previously the two could have disagreed once `m_maxRecordedHeight` diverged from `currentTipHeight`). TDD: new `record_stays_bounded_under_many_increasing_future_heights` (a much cheaper, non-flaky regression guard than a timing assertion, since the fix changes `size()`'s own return value directly -- confirmed RED against the pre-fix code, `10000 >= 300`). Mutation-tested: reverting to plain `currentTipHeight` and removing the `m_maxRecordedHeight` update both independently reproduce the RED state. **MEDIUM**: the header comment's "between them already cover [every announcement path]" claim was false -- `CMPCTBLOCK` announcing a block whose parent isn't known yet (`net_processing.cpp`) hit an early `return true` before any availability call at all, unlike the structurally-identical HEADERS-unconnecting case which at least parks the hash in `hashLastUnknownBlock` for later resolution. Rated MEDIUM not HIGH by the reviewer's own reasoning (accepted directly): the same early return also means this peer never becomes a download source for that block on the strength of this message, so an accountability-based fetch would not have chosen it anyway -- but the "closed set" framing itself was still a real overclaim. **Fixed** by mirroring the HEADERS-unconnecting pattern exactly (`UpdateBlockAvailability(pfrom->GetId(), cmpctblock.header.GetHash())` before the early return, same `cs_main` scope already held there); `announcerring.h`'s own doc comment corrected from "closed" to "closed as of 2.2.3b/F-153, not closed permanently," with a note that any future announcement mechanism (e.g. a commitment-only relay message) is a new insertion point this comment cannot already know about. **MEDIUM, test-quality**: two mutants on `Record`'s OWN eviction/insertion-guard boundary (`<` to `<=` on the cutoff comparison; `cutoff = tip-depth` to `tip-depth-1`) survived the original 8 tests -- those only independently pinned `WasAnnounced`'s own boundary, not `Record`'s. Neither mutant made `WasAnnounced` return a wrong answer (confirming that function's own re-derived-freshness property held regardless), so these were memory-hygiene off-by-ones, not correctness bugs -- but the suite's own implicit claim of a closed `[tip-depth, tip]` window was only actually pinned on one side. **Fixed** with two new boundary tests (an entry exactly at the cutoff must be kept; one exactly below it must be rejected at insertion) -- both independently confirmed to already pass against the pre-F-153 code (the implementation was correct; only the coverage was missing) and confirmed to fail against each of the two named mutants. **LOW**: `RecordAnnouncedHeaderRange`'s own loop-termination (walk `headers[0..]` until matching `pindexLast`'s hash) has no defined behaviour if that hash is never found -- structurally unreachable today (confirmed: `pindexLast` always comes from `AcceptBlockHeader`'s own out-param, assigned only from inside a loop over this exact `headers` vector), but not previously guarded. **Fixed** with a loud `LogPrint` (not a crash -- network-input-triggered crashes are their own DoS class, this project's own F-31/F-40 precedent) if the loop ever completes without finding a match, plus hoisting `header.GetHash()` to compute it once per iteration instead of twice. **LOW, doc accuracy**: `announcerring.h` now also notes the ring is destroyed with its `CNodeState` on disconnect (`FinalizeNode`) -- a peer that reconnects starts with an empty ring, the same residual-limitation shape F-143 already accepted for a silent withholder. Full mutation re-verification (2 new mutants for the HIGH fix, both die) on a genuine clean rebuild. 3 new tests. Full suite: 546 cases (`!miner_tests`) clean, up from 543; `miner_tests` alone unchanged (the one known pre-existing failure) | Fable adversarial review of commit 69d5607aa, 2026-09-23; the HIGH's own mechanism re-derived from source before accepting the reviewer's framing, and BOTH the pre-fix (692s) and post-fix (81ms) timings independently reproduced via a temporary scratch probe rather than trusted from the review's own numbers alone; mutation-tested (2 new mutants for the HIGH, both die; the two pre-existing boundary mutants from the review's own report re-confirmed to now be caught) | **fixed 2026-09-23** |
| F-154 | **A second, targeted Fable review of F-153's own fix (commit 40442c8b1) converges -- no HIGH, no remaining bug. Confirmed `m_maxRecordedHeight` genuinely updates BEFORE the cutoff is computed within the same call (the correct order -- verified by reading, then by the LOW-1 mutant below), and that `WasAnnounced` genuinely shares the same `EffectiveHeight` basis as `Record` (no possible disagreement between what's kept and what's reported fresh). Confirmed the `CMPCTBLOCK` fix's `cs_main` scope precisely (held from `net_processing.cpp`'s own lock open to the `return true`, traced line by line, not assumed from a comment) and that it introduces no reentrancy issue (`UpdateBlockAvailability`/`ProcessBlockAvailability`/`RecordAnnouncer` take no lock but `cs_main` itself). Confirmed both new boundary tests from F-153 (eviction-boundary-kept, one-below-boundary-rejected) genuinely kill their own named mutants, independently re-verified rather than re-trusted. **MEDIUM, accepted as an inherent tradeoff, not fixed**: `EffectiveHeight`'s own fix -- correctly bounding the ring's memory/CPU cost by windowing around whichever is higher, the connected tip or the highest height ever recorded from a given peer -- has a real, previously-undocumented side effect: during any long catch-up, a peer's own header stream routinely races far ahead of where bodies are actually being fetched (headers-first sync accepts headers well ahead of body-fetch progress), so the ring evicts near-tip-from-the-node's-own-perspective entries the moment the SAME peer's header frontier passes `depth` blocks beyond them. Concrete scenario: a peer serves headers to 900,000 while the node is still fetching bodies near 500,000; by the time a fetch of body 500,005 fails, that peer's own ring has already evicted everything below ~899,710, so `WasAnnounced` answers `false` for a peer that genuinely did announce it. Judged inherent, not a bug to patch here: the height-bounded-memory requirement (F-143's own round-3 finding, rejecting a count-bounded ring as grindable) is fundamentally in tension with retaining IBD-scale history, and closing this would need a real design change (e.g. bounding relative to the node's OWN download-window progress rather than the peer's header frontier), not a fix to this type. **Fixed by documenting, not patching**: `announcerring.h`'s own top-level comment now states this residual explicitly, with the same concrete scenario, and instructs that 2.2.4's own design must not assume ring coverage during a long catch-up; `EffectiveHeight`'s own comment corrected to stop framing the fix as a pure "correctly degrading" win without the caveat; `ANNOUNCER_RING_DEPTH`'s comment (`net_processing.cpp`) corrected from "blocks behind the current tip" (no longer accurate) to "blocks behind the ring's own effective height." A related, secondary observation also now documented: `m_maxRecordedHeight` is peer-CONTROLLED, so a peer that gets one header more than `depth` blocks past its own prior frontier accepted evicts its own near-tip announcements for free -- but doing so costs real, chain-length-scale proof-of-work, not a cheap grind, so this is judged a different, acceptable class of thing from the count-bounded design (v3) F-143 rejected specifically for being cheaply gameable. **LOW**: the `m_maxRecordedHeight`-before-cutoff ordering (load-bearing per the HIGH fix's own correctness) was never independently pinned -- a mutant moving the update to after the cutoff computation survives all of F-153's own 11 tests (the effect is benign, a one-call eviction lag hidden by the size-bound test's own `<300` slack: 291 vs 292). **Fixed** with a new test recording two heights 11 apart at `depth=10` and asserting the first is evicted within the SAME call that records the second (not one call later) -- confirmed RED against the ordering mutant, GREEN on the real code. **LOW**: the `RecordAnnouncedHeaderRange` invariant-violation guard (F-153) used `LogPrint(BCLog::NET, ...)`, silent by default without `-debug=net`, despite its own comment saying "log loudly." **Fixed** by switching to `LogPrintf` (unconditional), matching this file's own existing convention for a genuinely unusual condition worth unconditional operator visibility (several precedents already in `net_processing.cpp`, e.g. the whitelisted/manually-connected-peer misbehaviour warnings). **LOW**: a literal, uninterpreted `&lt;` HTML-entity artifact in a test comment (`test/announcerring_tests.cpp`), corrected to a plain `<`. | F-155 | **2.2.4 built: the single-source GETBODYRANGE fetch client and the body-ARRIVAL write path -- `ProcessFetchedBodyRange` (`validation.h`/`.cpp`), following F-133/F-135's crash-ordering discipline applied to a second call site, per F-139's own spec and build-plan.md's 2.2.4 row** | **The write path**: `bool CChainState::ProcessFetchedBodyRange(CBlockIndex *pindexNew, const CCommitmentBlock &commitments, const std::vector<CTransactionRef> &bodies, CValidationState &state, const CChainParams &chainparams)`, `EXCLUSIVE_LOCKS_REQUIRED(cs_main)`. Guard first: a no-op (returns false, `state.IsValid()`) unless `BLOCK_HAVE_DATA` is set and bodies are not already held (`HaveBodies`) -- a second, independent filter behind the caller's own in-flight bookkeeping, not the only one. `MaterialiseBlock` (already existed, unwired until now -- `primitives/block.cpp`) validates every body's hash against `commitments.vCommitments[i]`; a mismatch is the ANSWERING PEER's fault, not the block's, so it's reported via `state.DoS(20, ..., corruptionIn=true, ...)`, never `BLOCK_FAILED_VALID`. On a hash match, runs `CheckBlock`/`ContextualCheckBlock` -- exactly the two functions F-139's own spec named, reused unchanged, not reinvented -- with `fCheckPOW=false` (already verified when the header itself was accepted) and `fCheckMerkleRoot=false` (PROVABLY redundant: `MaterialiseBlock`'s own per-tx hash check makes the materialised block's merkle root identical to the identifier-list root a commitment-only accept already verified over the same ordered hash list). A genuine body-dependent failure here (negative-value outputs, bad sigop/input-count/size budgets, anything `CheckCommitmentBlock`'s coinbase-only view can't see) IS a fact about the block: `state.IsInvalid() && !state.CorruptionPossible()` sets `BLOCK_FAILED_VALID`, mirroring `AcceptBlock`'s own gate exactly. Then F-133/F-135's write-before-claim ordering, applied here for the first time to a body record that was NEVER written (unlike the existing `ReceivedBlockBodies`, which assumes `SaveBodyToDisk` already ran unconditionally at initial accept -- the only shape the `-perfwithholdbody` harness produces): `SaveBodyToDisk` (the file's own existing static helper, reused) writes the real bytes FIRST; only once that succeeds are `nBodyFile`/`nBodyPos` set and `BLOCK_HAVE_BODY_RECORD` raised, together, unconditionally (F-135's own corrected semantics: this bit means "we know where these bytes are," true the instant the write succeeds); `RecordBodyPositionByHash` updates the hash-keyed index immediately (F-139's own design: hash-keyed populates at record-write time, height-keyed only at connect/disconnect for the active chain -- unaffected here, driven by the existing connect-time mechanism once the caller's own `ActivateBestChain` call runs); the rest (`BLOCK_HAVE_BODIES`, the descendant-unparking walk, the index's own re-affirmation) delegates to the EXISTING `ReceivedBlockBodies`, not duplicated. **The fetch client** (`bodyrange.h`/`.cpp`, matching this project's established split between pure/testable logic and untested `net_processing.cpp` glue): `ValidateBodyRangeResponse(request, response)` -- wire-shape only (hash/index echo, no over-delivery, no null body entries; an empty `vBodies` always passes as an honest miss) -- does NOT know whether this exact request was ever sent (the caller's in-flight bookkeeping's job) and does NOT validate body content (`MaterialiseBlock`'s job). `ShouldRequestBodyRange(fWasAnnounced, nNow, nNextAttempt, nInFlight, nMaxInFlight)` -- pure eligibility: announced by THIS peer (`CAnnouncerRing::WasAnnounced`, now wired to production for the first time -- previously unused per F-152-154), past its own backoff deadline, under the aggregate cap; checked in that order, cap last since it's a global resource limit independent of this specific peer/hash pair. `NextBodyRetryBackoffMicros(nAttempts, nBaseMicros, nMaxMicros)` -- the exact arithmetic 1.3.6/H-2's original whole-block `g_body_retry_state` site used inline (`nBaseMicros << min(nAttempts-1, 5U)`, clamped), factored out and reused verbatim, not reinvented, per build-plan.md's own explicit "not new backoff logic" instruction. `DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_INFLIGHT = 16` (bodyrange.h, shared with init.cpp's help text, matching `DEFAULT_CHECKBLOCKS`'s own cross-file placement). **`g_body_retry_state` re-keyed** from bare `uint256` to `std::pair<NodeId, uint256>` (`net_processing.cpp`) -- B3's own real remaining gap per build-plan.md: the mechanism's job (a body that keeps getting requested without resolving) moved from the whole-block MSG_BLOCK path (now only reachable via `-perfwithholdcount`, since a real MSG_BLOCK delivery always carries bodies) to the new single-source GETBODYRANGE path, which is genuinely per-peer -- one peer's failure to deliver must not back off a DIFFERENT peer that also announced the same block. **`net_processing.cpp` wiring**: `FindNextBlocksToDownload` gained a `vBodyBlocks` out-param, filled alongside the existing `vToDownload` by the same ancestor walk -- a commitment-known/body-missing block now routes to the new GETBODYRANGE path instead of a redundant whole-block GETDATA that would re-fetch commitments it already has; a new "Message: getbodyrange" `SendMessages` section (gated `-fetchbodyrange`, off by default, matching `-servebodyrange`'s own precedent -- this path has not run against a live/exposed network) issues single-source requests (`mapBodyRangeInFlight` keyed by hash: at most one request per block at a time, across all peers) and resumes multi-chunk continuations from `mapBodyRangePartial`; a new `BODYRANGE` dispatch arm matches a response against THIS SPECIFIC peer's own in-flight entry (a response from anyone else is silently ignored as stale/unsolicited, not misbehaviour -- could be a genuinely late answer to an abandoned request), validates shape, accumulates chunks, and on completion calls `ProcessFetchedBodyRange` then `ActivateBestChain`. **A real resource-leak bug found and fixed while building this, before any commit**: `FinalizeNode` freed `mapBlocksInFlight` on peer disconnect but had no equivalent for `mapBodyRangeInFlight`/`nBodyRangeInFlight` -- an outstanding GETBODYRANGE request against a peer that disconnects would otherwise permanently strand that block's fetch AND leak one slot off the aggregate cap forever. Fixed: `FinalizeNode` now erases every `mapBodyRangeInFlight` entry naming the disconnecting peer and decrements the cap counter; two new asserts (`mapBodyRangeInFlight.empty()`, `nBodyRangeInFlight == 0`) confirm this holds once every peer is gone, mirroring the existing `mapBlocksInFlight`-derived asserts in the same function. `mapBodyRangePartial` is deliberately left untouched on disconnect -- bodies already received and shape-validated from the disconnecting peer are still good for whichever peer resumes the fetch. Config: `-maxbodyrangeinflight=<n>` (default `DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_INFLIGHT`) and `-fetchbodyrange` (default off) registered in `init.cpp`, both `DEBUG_ONLY`/`DEBUG_TEST` matching `-servebodyrange`'s own convention. **D-156 (this build's own recorded decision)**: `ProcessFetchedBodyRange` does not itself cross-check `commitments.GetHash()` against `pindexNew`'s own true header hash -- it trusts the caller to supply a consistent pair. Safe in production today: the only real caller (`net_processing.cpp`'s `BODYRANGE` handler) always sources `commitments` via `ReadCommitmentBlockFromDisk(commitments, pindex, ...)`, which reads BY `pindex`'s own stored position, guaranteeing consistency by construction -- there is no code path that could pass a mismatched pair. Flagged, not fixed, because adding the check would be pure defense-in-depth with no reachable production bug behind it (verified: no other call site exists), and every existing test that deliberately supplies a mismatched pair to exercise `ProcessFetchedBodyRange`'s own error handling in isolation (this build's own convention, matching `SimulateBodyRecordNeverWritten`'s "clobber to a state only a future real path would produce" precedent already established in this file) would need rewriting if the check were added. Plus: the test premise bug F-155's own missing `BLOCK_FAILED_VALID` test hit, found and fixed; the full mutation-testing record for both commits** | **The gap**: F-155's `FinalizeNode` fix frees a `mapBodyRangeInFlight` slot on disconnect, and a matching `BODYRANGE` response frees it on resolution -- but a peer that stays CONNECTED and simply never answers at all is caught by neither. Left unreaped, a single silently-unresponsive peer permanently narrows the aggregate cap by one slot for as long as it stays connected -- across enough such peers, exhausts it. **Fixed**: `bool IsBodyRangeRequestStale(int64_t nRequestTime, int64_t nNow, int64_t nStaleAfterMicros)` (`bodyrange.h`/`.cpp`), pure, matching `ShouldRequestBodyRange`'s own established split -- `nNow - nRequestTime > nStaleAfterMicros`, strictly greater so a request exactly at the threshold is not yet stale (a deliberately different boundary convention from `ShouldRequestBodyRange`'s own "equal passes" backoff check, since this asks "has a DURATION been exceeded," not "has a DEADLINE passed" -- both self-consistent, documented as such). `net_processing.cpp`'s `SendMessages` now sweeps `mapBodyRangeInFlight` for stale entries (reusing the EXISTING `BODY_RETRY_MAX_MICROS` backoff ceiling as the staleness threshold -- "longer than the longest gap this mechanism would ever wait between its own retries" is already an established notion of "too long" here, matching build-plan.md's "not new backoff logic" instruction in spirit even though this isn't backoff arithmetic itself) on EVERY peer's pass, not gated on that peer's own eligibility, since a stale slot can be held against any peer while a different one is calling `SendMessages`; `mapBodyRangePartial` is left alone, matching `FinalizeNode`'s own convention. 4 new tests (fresh request stands, exact-boundary not-yet-stale, one-micro-past-boundary reaped, long-silent reaped). **Test premise bug found and fixed** (before any commit, via a real failing run, not caught by inspection): the first draft of `process_fetched_body_range_fails_the_block_on_a_genuine_body_dependent_violation` (`acceptancebit_tests.cpp`) tried to get an invalid block's own `pindex` into the index by constructing a block with a negative-value output and accepting it via `ProcessNewBlock` under `PerfWithholdGuard` -- this fails `BOOST_REQUIRE` every time, because `AcceptBlock` unconditionally runs the FULL `CheckBlock`/`ContextualCheckBlock` against today's only real local `CBlock` regardless of withholding (`PerfWithholdGuard` only defers the body WRITE/claim, not validation -- confirmed directly, `validation.cpp`'s own comment on this: "not actually before any per-transaction work runs on today's only real path"). There is no way, with today's test infrastructure (or today's production code -- F-105 is still open), to get a genuinely-invalid, never-locally-validated commitment-only `pindex` into the index. **Fixed** by decoupling: accept a real VALID block first (realistic chain context for `ContextualCheckBlock`), then hand `ProcessFetchedBodyRange` a `commitments`/`bodies` pair that is internally self-consistent (`MaterialiseBlock` passes) but commits to a structurally invalid transaction instead of the block's real one -- the same latitude the existing hash-mismatch test already takes (a deliberately mismatched pair), breaking the other half of the pairing instead (see F-155's own D-156 on why `ProcessFetchedBodyRange` permits this). **Mutation record, both commits, all independently applied/reverted against a genuine incremental rebuild** (9 mutations, 8 killed on first try, 1 correctly classified as structurally inert rather than forced into a fake test): `ValidateBodyRangeResponse`'s hash-equality check inverted -- 3 tests fail, killed. | F-157 | **Fable review of F-155/F-156 (commits 80507d58a, 74a1e946e) found one CONFIRMED HIGH bug (`cs_main` held across an `ActivateBestChain` call, this file's own documented-and-elsewhere-honoured contract), one real staller-detection blind spot, and one real resource leak on peer disconnect -- all fixed, all verified, three straightforward cleanups folded in per the reviewer's own invitation (matching F-147/F-148's precedent for mixing severities in one finding)** | **HIGH, CONFIRMED, fixed**: the `BODYRANGE` response handler (`net_processing.cpp`) took a bare, unscoped `LOCK(cs_main);` that stayed held through the whole handler, including the `::ChainstateActive().ActivateBestChain(...)` call at the bottom -- `ActivateBestChain`'s own contract (`validation.h`'s doc; `AssertLockNotHeld(cs_main)` at both its free-function and `CChainState::` member definitions, `validation.cpp`) requires cs_main NOT be held, and this file's other two call sites (`ProcessGetData` ~1885, `ProcessGetBlocks` ~3322) already release it first, one with the identical `// release cs_main before calling ActivateBestChain` comment now reused verbatim here. **Fixed** by confining every cs_main-dependent step to its own `{ ... }` scope, recording only a `bool fShouldActivateBestChain` across the boundary, and calling `ActivateBestChain` after that scope's lock has closed -- exactly the established pattern, not a new one. **Verified, not just pattern-matched**: `AssertLockNotHeld` compiles to a total no-op without `DEBUG_LOCKORDER` (`sync.h`'s own `#else` branch), which this project's build does not default on, so the runtime assert alone would never have caught this in a normal test run. Rather than a full `DEBUG_LOCKORDER` reconfigure + a from-scratch P2P dispatch test harness (net_processing.cpp's `ProcessMessage`/`SendMessages` have no existing unit-test infrastructure driving them at all, confirmed by grep, matching this tree's own repeatedly-reaffirmed convention, F-149/F-152), added `LOCKS_EXCLUDED(cs_main)` (Clang thread-safety annotation, `threadsafety.h`'s own existing GCC-no-op/Clang-enforced split, already used throughout this codebase for `GUARDED_BY`/`EXCLUSIVE_LOCKS_REQUIRED`) to both `ActivateBestChain` declarations in `validation.h` -- a genuine, permanent, compile-time-checked strengthening of the documented contract, not test-only scaffolding. Empirically RED/GREEN verified with `clang++ -fsyntax-only -Wthread-safety` against the real translation unit (matching this project's own compile flags, extracted from `make -n`): reintroducing the exact bug (removing just the two scope braces) produces a real `net_processing.cpp:3655:34: warning: cannot call function 'ActivateBestChain' while mutex 'cs_main' is held [-Wthread-safety-analysis]`; the fix produces none. This diagnostic is now a standing, automatic check for ANY future caller of either `ActivateBestChain` overload under Clang, not a one-off probe. **A second thread-safety gap found via the same tooling, fixed alongside**: the new `EraseBodyRangeInFlight` helper (added by this same finding's cleanup #2 below) read/wrote `cs_main`-guarded globals without its own `EXCLUSIVE_LOCKS_REQUIRED(cs_main)` annotation -- every real call site already holds the lock (verified), but Clang couldn't see that without the annotation and flagged it; added, confirmed clean. **MEDIUM, real, fixed**: `FindNextBlocksToDownload`'s own staller-detection (`vBlocks.size() == 0 && waitingfor != nodeid`, both occurrences) never consulted the new `vBodyBlocks` output 2.2.4 added -- a peer with genuine outstanding body-range work queued (found earlier in the SAME ancestor walk) could still cause an unrelated peer (`waitingfor`) to be wrongly marked `nStallingSince`, eventually risking a wrongful `BLOCK_STALLING_TIMEOUT` disconnect for a peer that did nothing wrong, purely because the WHOLE-BLOCK candidate list happened to be empty. **Fixed**: extracted `bool HasOutstandingBlockDownloadWork(size_t nWholeBlockCandidates, size_t nBodyRangeCandidates)` (`bodyrange.h`/`.cpp`, pure, matching this project's own established split) -- `true` when EITHER candidate list has entries; both call sites now gate on `!HasOutstandingBlockDownloadWork(vBlocks.size(), vBodyBlocks.size())` instead of `vBlocks.size() == 0` alone. 4 new tests. **MEDIUM, real, fixed**: `g_body_retry_state` (re-keyed to `(NodeId, hash)` by F-155) is only ever erased when THAT SAME peer's later `FindNextBlocksToDownload` call resolves the block -- a peer that disconnects first never gets that later call, and `NodeId`s are never reused for the life of the process (`CConnman`'s own monotonic counter), so every (peer, hash) pair a departed peer ever backed off stayed in the map forever, growing unboundedly with ordinary peer churn (each connect/disconnect/reconnect mints a fresh `NodeId`). The exact same leak class F-155/F-156 already closed for `mapBodyRangeInFlight`, now closed for its sibling map: `FinalizeNode` sweeps `g_body_retry_state` for every entry naming the departing peer; a new `assert(g_body_retry_state.empty())` added alongside the existing `mapBodyRangeInFlight`/`nBodyRangeInFlight` asserts once `mapNodeState.empty()`, exercised for real by the EXISTING `denialofservice_tests.cpp` cases that already drive `FinalizeNode` down to the last peer (confirmed by grep -- 6 existing call sites across that file). **Three cleanups, folded in per the reviewer's own invitation, none touching behaviour**: (1) the "bodies wanted" one-liner (`pindex->nTx > 0 ? nTx-1 : 0`), duplicated at the `BODYRANGE` handler and the getbodyrange-issuing `SendMessages` section, factored into `static unsigned int BodyRangeWantedCount(const CBlockIndex *pindex)`. (2) the three near-identical "erase a `mapBodyRangeInFlight` entry, decrement `nBodyRangeInFlight`" sites (`FinalizeNode`, the stale-reap loop, the `BODYRANGE` handler's two single-entry erases) factored into `EraseBodyRangeInFlight(iterator)`, returning `map::erase`'s own next-iterator so an erase-while-iterating loop reads identically to calling `.erase()` directly -- the invariant (`mapBodyRangeInFlight.size()` staying in lockstep with `nBodyRangeInFlight`) can no longer drift at one site without the others. (3) the stale-in-flight-request reap loop ran once per CONNECTED PEER's own `SendMessages` pass rather than once per round, rescanning the same (small, but non-zero) map N times under `cs_main` for N peers with no additional benefit, since staleness is a function of real time, not of which peer happens to be calling -- throttled to `BODY_RANGE_MAINTENANCE_INTERVAL_MICROS` (1s, far finer than the 30s staleness threshold itself needs) via a new `nBodyRangeMaintenanceLastRun` timestamp. 5 new tests total (4 `HasOutstandingBlockDownloadWork` cases in `bodyrange_tests.cpp`; the reap-throttle and disconnect-sweep fixes have no dedicated new test, matching this tree's own established net_processing.cpp-glue convention -- verified by direct reading plus the pre-existing `denialofservice_tests.cpp` exercise noted above). **Mutation-tested**: `HasOutstandingBlockDownloadWork`'s `\|\|` narrowed to `&&` -- 2 tests fail, killed.
