| F-217 | **1.4 audited: two of its three named pieces were already fully built elsewhere and needed no new code; the third (K-7) needed D-14's own "one fork" bundling corrected before it could be built at all, since a receive-side, per-connection byte cap has none of the synchronized-activation property that bundling assumed. Built as a plain constant change, no `EUpdate`/height-gating — and along the way, found and closed a genuine, previously-uncited structural ceiling (`serialize.h:MAX_SIZE`) that would otherwise have made any raise past 32 MiB silently inert and, worse, unsafe on the sending side.** | **1. The "8 MB cap" part was already built, under 1.2/4.6, not this row.** 1.4's own text described "the 8 MB `MaxBlockSize` (K-1)" riding the fork; what 1.2/D-19 actually shipped is a dual byte+input-count budget — `consensus/consensus.h:COMMITMENT_BUDGET_BODY_BYTES` (110,000,000) and `consensus/consensus.h:COMMITMENT_BUDGET_MAX_INPUTS` (700,000, F-98) — sized to D-19's own 1,500 tx/s realistic-traffic design point, not a literal 8 MB constant. `consensus/consensus.h:MaxBlockSize`'s `fCommitmentBudgetActive` branch returns `COMMITMENT_BUDGET_BODY_BYTES` directly; `g_commitmentBudgetActive` is pinned per block height by `validation.h:CommitmentModeAtHeight` (F-210) from the real `EUpdate::COMMITMENT_MODE` bit (`update/update.h`), registered on devnet/regtest by F-214 — the SAME bit D-14's "one fork" language anticipated. Nothing is left to build: the mechanism is live, tested (`blockbudget_tests.cpp`, F-98), and activates on the identical schedule 1.4 described, just under a different row's own byline and at ~14x the row's stated figure (110,000,000 vs. 8,000,000). **2. The "2 MB by policy" part needed nothing — confirmed, not assumed.** `git diff master -- src/policy/policy.h` on this branch shows zero touches to `policy/policy.h:DEFAULT_BLOCK_MAX_SIZE` (still `2000000`); the only edit this branch ever made to that file is `DEFAULT_MAX_MEMPOOL_SIZE` (F-199, a different constant entirely). An untouched upstream default, not a gap. **3. K-7's synchronized-activation question, verified independently, not taken on F-213's word.** F-213 (4.6's own audit) already found `net.h:MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH` lacks `EUpdate`'s synchronized-activation-height property; this task re-derived the same conclusion directly from `net.cpp:ReceiveMsgBytes` (`net.cpp:663`, the oversized-message disconnect: `if (msg.in_data && msg.hdr.nMessageSize > MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH) { ...; return false; }`) — a receive-side, per-connection, purely local check with no per-height gating anywhere in the surrounding function or its callers (confirmed by reading `net.h`/`net.cpp` in full, not assuming). Two peers running different values simply degrade (one refuses a message size the other would have sent); neither can accept a block or transaction the other rejects on this basis alone, unlike an unsynchronized `MaxBlockSize` raise, where one node accepting an oversized block while another rejects it is a chain split. D-14's "ride the same activation" framing is therefore wrong for this one piece, confirmed independently rather than inherited. **4. What K-7's raise is actually FOR, re-derived from source: a throughput question, not a correctness one, since 4.5.2.** `docs/build-plan.md`'s own post-table prose (pre-correction) cited a ~23 MB worst-case `BLOCKTXN` at the 2 MB design point, already exceeding the old 3 MiB limit and disconnecting the sender — but F-200/F-201/F-202 (4.5.2) already redesigned this exact scenario: `net_processing.cpp:SendBlockTransactions` sums the requested set's real serialized size and, if it exceeds `net_processing.cpp:MAX_BLOCKTXN_RESPONSE_BYTES` (itself derived from `MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH`), **declines and logs, sending nothing** — no disconnect, no full-block fallback (F-202 found and removed that fallback: the requested set is a subset of the block, so if the subset doesn't fit, the full block provably doesn't either) — leaving the requester's own pre-existing block-download stall/timeout machinery (`BLOCK_STALLING_TIMEOUT`/`BLOCK_DOWNLOAD_TIMEOUT_BASE`/`_PER_PEER`, `validation.h`) to recover, exactly as it already does for any other block that never arrives. This path is safe at ANY `MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH` value, including today's — so raising K-7 cannot be fixing a correctness gap that no longer exists; it can only be avoiding an unnecessary fallback to that slower recovery path for legitimately-sized-but-large requests, a throughput question. **5. The real, previously-uncited limit on how far K-7 can usefully go: `serialize.h:MAX_SIZE`.** Read `net.cpp:CNetMessage::readHeader` in full: `if (hdr.nMessageSize > MAX_SIZE) return -1;` (`net.cpp:737`) fires **before** `net.cpp:663`'s own `MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH` check is ever reached, on every message, unconditionally — `serialize.h:MAX_SIZE` (`0x02000000`, 32 MiB) is `ReadCompactSize`'s (`serialize.h:432`) own universal sanity cap on every CompactSize-prefixed field anywhere in this codebase's wire format, reused here as `CMessageHeader`'s own declared-size gate (confirmed also live at `protocol.cpp:CMessageHeader::IsValid`, a second, independent enforcement of the identical bound). Raising `MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH` past `MAX_SIZE` would therefore be **silently inert on receive** (`readHeader`'s own gate rejects first, so `net.cpp:663`'s check — and the whole point of raising K-7 — would never be reached for any message actually big enough to need it) and **actively unsafe on send**: `net_processing.cpp:SendBlockTransactions` would compute "under the ceiling" against the inflated constant and queue a genuinely oversized outgoing `BLOCKTXN`, which the RECEIVING peer's own `readHeader` then rejects, disconnecting over exactly the scenario F-202's redesign existed to avoid — reintroducing the original bug via a different, less obvious route. This is a genuine structural ceiling this task's own brief did not anticipate (it assumed a "straightforward constant change" with no other bound in play) and no prior finding names — confirmed directly by reading `net.cpp`/`serialize.h`, not inferred from `bodystore.h`'s own pre-existing citation of the SAME constant (`bodystore.h:131`, a coincidental prior collision with the identical 32 MiB ceiling in the body-store record format, unrelated to this constant). **6. Built.** `net.h:MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH` raised from `3 * 1024 * 1024` to `MAX_SIZE - 4096` (33,550,336) — the largest value that is not silently inert, with the 4096 B margin reusing this codebase's own established convention (`net_processing.cpp:MAX_BLOCKTXN_RESPONSE_MARGIN`/`DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_BYTES`'s static_asserts use the identical value) so `net.cpp:663`'s own check stays the genuinely binding one rather than degenerating into dead code shadowed by `MAX_SIZE`'s own gate. A new `static_assert(MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH < MAX_SIZE, ...)` (`net.h`) pins the relationship permanently — mutation-tested by temporarily setting the constant to `MAX_SIZE` exactly (no margin): the build itself fails at this static_assert, a stronger catch than a runtime test. Every pre-existing static_assert this constant feeds (`net_processing.cpp:DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_BYTES`/`MAX_STANDARD_TX_SIZE`'s own two asserts tying them to `MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH`) re-verified to still hold at the new, larger value by a clean rebuild succeeding. No `EUpdate` bit, no height-gating — confirmed unnecessary by point 3, so none was added. **7. Tests.** `net_tests.cpp`: `protocol_message_length_is_max_size_minus_the_established_margin` pins the exact chosen value against `serialize.h:MAX_SIZE` minus a hardcoded literal margin — NOT against `MAX_PROTOCOL_MESSAGE_LENGTH` itself, a self-reference trap this task's own first draft fell into and caught by actually running it (three boundary tests referencing the constant symbolically passed unchanged against the pre-fix 3 MiB value, giving zero signal that the raise had happened at all — the literal-pinned test is the one that gave genuine RED, `3145728 != 33550336`, confirmed by temporarily reverting `net.h` alone via a `git stash` and rebuilding before restoring). `protocol_message_length_accepts_exactly_at_the_new_ceiling`/`_disconnects_one_byte_over_the_new_ceiling` exercise the real `CNode::ReceiveMsgBytes` path (not the lower-level `CNetMessage::readHeader`/`readData` calls a bare unit test might reach for) with only a 24-byte declared header, no payload bytes needed even at a tens-of-megabytes boundary, since `net.cpp:663`'s check fires the instant `msg.in_data` becomes true. `protocol_message_length_stays_strictly_below_the_independent_max_size_gate` proves `serialize.h:MAX_SIZE`'s own independent gate is still live, not assumed. Mutation-checked: `net.cpp:663`'s `>` weakened to `>=` is caught by the exactly-at-ceiling test (reverted, reconfirmed GREEN). `blocktxn_tests.cpp`'s pre-existing `getblocktxn_over_derived_ceiling_declines_without_full_block_fallback` needed updating, not just re-running: its 40-tx (~3.5 MB) fixture no longer exceeds the new, ~10x larger derived ceiling, so it stopped exercising the decline path at all (confirmed by running it unmodified post-fix: `critical check requested > DERIVED_BLOCKTXN_CEILING has failed`, a genuine, observed regression the raise would otherwise have shipped silently). Fixed by scaling to `numTx=400` (~35.7 MB, safely past the new ceiling, still comfortably under `COMMITMENT_BUDGET_BODY_BYTES`/`COMMITMENT_BUDGET_MAX_INPUTS`) — which itself now exceeds the pre-existing, unrelated `consensus/consensus.h:MAX_DIP0001_BLOCK_SIZE` (8,000,000) legacy cap, needing a genuinely active `EUpdate::COMMITMENT_MODE` at the test block's own height (`CommitmentModeAtHeight`, F-210, overrides any bare `g_commitmentBudgetActive` assignment made before `ProcessNewBlock` runs) — closed by duplicating `acceptancebit_tests.cpp`'s own established `SetCommitmentModeActiveForTest`/`CommitmentBudgetGuard` pattern locally, matching this file's own existing convention of not sharing test-only helpers across files. **8. Full suite.** Green except the one known pre-existing `miner_tests/CreateNewBlock_validity` failure, confirmed via a freshly-rebuilt binary (mtime checked before trusting the run, per this task's own warning about a stale binary giving a false pass) | `source` — direct read of `net.h`/`net.cpp` in full (`ReceiveMsgBytes`, `CNetMessage::readHeader`/`readData`), `serialize.h` (`MAX_SIZE`, `ReadCompactSize`), `protocol.cpp:CMessageHeader::IsValid`, `net_processing.cpp`'s `SendBlockTransactions`/`MAX_BLOCKTXN_RESPONSE_BYTES`/`MAX_BLOCKTXN_RESPONSE_MARGIN`/`DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_BYTES` and their existing static_asserts, `consensus/consensus.h` (`MaxBlockSize`, `COMMITMENT_BUDGET_BODY_BYTES`, `COMMITMENT_BUDGET_MAX_INPUTS`, `MAX_DIP0001_BLOCK_SIZE`), `policy/policy.h:DEFAULT_BLOCK_MAX_SIZE`, `git diff master -- src/policy/policy.h`; `docs/findings.md` F-98/F-200/F-201/F-202/F-210/F-213/F-214 read in full; `docs/build-plan.md`'s own post-table "23 MB at 2 MB design point" prose re-derived against the actual shipped D-19 caps rather than re-quoted; TDD (genuine RED via a `git stash`-isolated `net.h` revert, confirmed GREEN on restore), mutation-checking (`net.cpp:663`'s `>`→`>=`, caught; `net.h`'s margin removed entirely, caught at compile time by the new static_assert), full suite run clean before and after | **built 2026-09-29 — 1.4 needed no new code for two of its three named pieces (already done under 1.2/4.6 and an untouched default, respectively); K-7's raise is built as a standalone, non-consensus constant change, bounded by a newly-cited structural ceiling (`serialize.h:MAX_SIZE`) this task's own brief did not anticipate** |
| F-218 | **2.2's genuinely open gap closed: a real per-connection, serving-side GETBODYRANGE budget, plus a naming-drift correction (F-143's deferred per-connection budget was never "2.2.3b" -- F-152 built the announcer ring under that name, orphaning the budget's own stale cross-reference) and the end-to-end functional test F-159 flagged as still missing.** | **1. The naming-drift, confirmed by reading, not assumed.** `net_processing.cpp`'s own `DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_BYTES` doc comment said the per-connection records-and-bytes budget was "still deferred to 2.2.3b" -- but F-152's own row title says outright "2.2.3b built: the announcer ring" (`announcerring.h`/`.cpp`, `CNodeState::announcerRing`), and F-153's own fix corrects `announcerring.h`'s header comment from "closed" to "closed as of 2.2.3b/F-153" -- both confirm 2.2.3b was assigned to the announcer ring, not the budget. F-143's own spec (round 3+) bundled BOTH as deferred scope items under one paragraph ("a real server-side budget... folded into `-maxuploadtarget`" alongside the ring), and when the ring alone got built and labelled 2.2.3b, the budget's own citation was left pointing at a sub-step that now means something else -- an orphaned reference, not a real dependency. Corrected in place (`net_processing.cpp`'s doc comment no longer cites 2.2.3b for this). **2. What was genuinely open, confirmed against F-159's own closing sentence**: "The real per-connection BUDGET (as opposed to the ring, 2.2.3a's own still-open MEDIUM) remains unbuilt" -- F-150's MEDIUM finding (no per-message-type limit protects `GETBODYRANGE` the way `net_processing.cpp:MAX_BLOCKTXN_DEPTH` protects `GETBLOCKTXN`) was never actually closed by any of F-151 through F-159, confirmed by grep (`net.h`/`net_processing.cpp` had no `nBodyRange.*[Bb]udget` symbol before this finding). **3. Built: two token buckets on `CNode` itself (`net.h`), not `CNodeState`.** `net.h:MAX_BODYRANGE_SERVE_REQUESTS_TOKEN_BUCKET`/`net.h:MAX_BODYRANGE_SERVE_BYTES_TOKEN_BUCKET` cap two independent per-connection buckets (`net.h`'s `nBodyRangeServeRequestTokens`/`nBodyRangeServeByteTokens`/`nBodyRangeServeTokenTimestamp`), matching `net.h:MAX_ADDR_PROCESSING_TOKEN_BUCKET`'s own already-established token-bucket shape (`CNode::addrTokenBucket`) rather than inventing a new mechanism -- continuous refill by real elapsed time (`bodyrange.h:RefillServeBudgetTokens`), never a fixed-window reset (`net.cpp:CConnman::RecordBytesSent`'s own cycle-reset shape was considered and rejected: a token bucket has no reset-boundary edge case for a peer to burst around). Deliberately on `CNode`, never `CNodeState`/`mapNodeState` (`net_processing.cpp:CNodeState`, `EXCLUSIVE_LOCKS_REQUIRED(cs_main)`) -- the entire point of the `GETBODYRANGE` serving path (F-149, closing B4) is that it never touches `cs_main`, and putting this budget in `CNodeState` would have reopened that exact problem on every single request. **4. Why two buckets, not one, and why those two constants**: the request-count bucket (`net.h:MAX_BODYRANGE_SERVE_REQUESTS_TOKEN_BUCKET` = 128) bounds how many requests get processed regardless of outcome -- a `bodyrange.cpp:ValidateGetBodyRange` BAN classification (an out-of-range `nStartIndex`) still costs a real `ReadBodyRecordCount` disk read even though it sends zero response bytes, so a bytes-only budget would not bound that cost. The byte bucket (`net.h:MAX_BODYRANGE_SERVE_BYTES_TOKEN_BUCKET` = `consensus/consensus.h:COMMITMENT_BUDGET_BODY_BYTES` directly, not a re-typed literal -- F-202's own "no second constant to remember to update in step" lesson applied here too) bounds actual bytes sent, sized so one full bucket covers exactly one entire worst-case block body per refill interval. 128 is `ceil(110,000,000 / 1,048,576)` = 105, rounded up for headroom, matching `net_processing.cpp:DEFAULT_MAX_BODYRANGE_BYTES`'s own "fixed, generous default" style; the relationship between the two constants is pinned by a new `net_processing.cpp` `static_assert` (both constants and `consensus/consensus.h:COMMITMENT_BUDGET_BODY_BYTES` are only in scope together there) so a future change to either can't silently regress. Both buckets refill fully once per `net_processing.cpp`'s own `STALE_CHECK_INTERVAL` ("matches block interval") -- `GETBODYRANGE`'s own legitimate demand is tip-path-only (F-159), driven by new-block cadence, not a continuous stream. **5. Decline, not disconnect, for the new budget -- and the reverse for the pre-existing global cap, each matching its OWN best precedent rather than one invented uniform rule.** Exceeding either new token bucket declines and logs (`net_processing.cpp`'s `GETBODYRANGE` dispatch arm), matching `net_processing.cpp:SendBlockTransactions`/F-202's decline-and-recover shape: a per-connection budget that self-refills within one `STALE_CHECK_INTERVAL` doesn't warrant the same treatment as an actually-invalid (BAN) request, and disconnecting a peer merely for asking faster than the budget allows would punish a legitimately-fast honest catch-up peer for no defensive gain (it would simply reconnect). Separately, `net.cpp:CConnman::OutboundTargetReached`'s existing GLOBAL `-maxuploadtarget` cap was never previously consulted by this handler at all -- bytes were already counted automatically (`net.cpp:CConnman::RecordBytesSent`, generic across every message type via the socket-send path) but nothing gated the SERVING DECISION on it. Wired in now, matching `net_processing.cpp`'s own MEMPOOL-handler precedent for the IDENTICAL condition (`OutboundTargetReached(false) && !fWhitelisted`) -- including that precedent's own disconnect action, deliberately not reusing the new budget's decline-only shape: this is a different, pre-existing, operator-configured global resource this file already treats as disconnect-worthy everywhere else it's checked. **6. Byte charging is by ACTUAL response size, not a worst-case pre-charge.** `net_processing.cpp:SendBodyRange` now returns the response's real `GetSerializeSize`, matching `SendBlockTransactions`/F-202's own "measure the real cost" precedent; the byte bucket is allowed to go negative when spent (a "credit" pattern, `bodyrange.h:BodyRangeServeBudgetAvailable`'s own doc comment) so it self-corrects via ordinary refill rather than needing a pessimistic worst-case pre-charge that would unfairly refuse a small request merely because the bucket briefly reads under one full chunk's worth. **7. TDD.** `bodyrange.h:RefillServeBudgetTokens`/`bodyrange.h:BodyRangeServeBudgetAvailable` are pure, matching `bodyrange.h:ShouldRequestBodyRange`'s own established split (fully unit-tested, no `CNode`/`net_processing.cpp` scaffolding) -- 13 new `test/bodyrange_tests.cpp` cases, confirmed genuinely RED against a wrong stub (`return nCurrentTokens;`/`return true;`) before restoring the real implementation and reconfirming GREEN (79 cases, up from 66). `net_processing.cpp`'s own dispatch-arm wiring has no unit coverage, matching this tree's already-accepted convention for message-processing glue (`SENDCMPCT` the same way, F-149's own precedent) -- exercised instead by the new functional test (below). **8. F-159's own noted gap closed: `test/functional/feature_bodyrange_e2e.py`.** Confirmed by grep before writing it: neither `feature_body_refetch.py` nor `feature_body_refetch_backoff.py` ever sets `-fetchbodyrange`/`-servebodyrange` or mentions `GETBODYRANGE`/`BODYRANGE` -- both exercise the whole-block `GETDATA` fallback only. The new test runs two REAL node processes (a mocked `P2PDataStore` peer cannot exercise this: the real serving handler only exists in the C++ node) -- a server (`-commitmentblocks=1 -servebodyrange=1`) and a fetcher (`-fetchbodyrange=1`, plus `-perfwithholdheight`/`-perfwithholdcount=1` to force one genuine commitment-only/body-missing state) -- and proves, via `assert_debug_log` on BOTH sides ("received: bodyrange" on the fetcher, "received: getbodyrange" on the server), that a real wire round trip recovered the body, not the whole-block fallback. Also required giving the withheld block a real non-coinbase transaction: an empty block completes via F-158's own `nWanted==0` short-circuit with NO wire round trip at all, which would have made the test pass trivially. Registered in `test_runner.py` -- F-158's own lesson (an unregistered test is exactly how that regression shipped past a green C++ suite) makes registration part of closing the gap, not optional. This test's own single legitimate round trip also exercises the new budget's happy path directly (refill, gate, spend). **9. A real DEBUG_ONLY harness composition bug found while building the test -- worked around, not fixed, and why.** With `-perfwithholdcount >= 2`, the fetcher's OWN `GETBODYRANGE` fetch-completion path (`ProcessFetchedBodyRange`) writes the real body it received WITHOUT ever consulting `-perfwithholdcount`'s attempt counter (that path exists to recover a body a REAL peer withheld, not to participate in this harness's simulation) -- `HaveBodies()` becomes true while the harness's own count is still > 0, and `validation.cpp`'s own `ReadBlockFromDisk(CBlock&, const CBlockIndex*, ...)` overload's non-decrementing peek (`PerfWithholdStillActive`) then still reports "withheld" for the READ-BACK `ConnectTip` performs immediately afterward, which is a fatal `AbortNode` ("Failed to read block"), not a graceful decline -- reproduced directly (count=2, before settling on count=1). A fix deferring `PerfWithholdStillActive` to `HaveBodies()` was tried and reverted: it silently breaks three existing, deliberately-designed corruption-simulation tests (`test/acceptancebit_tests.cpp:verifydb_stops_at_a_bodies_gap_instead_of_reporting_corruption`/`node_round_voting_getvote_respects_have_bodies_instead_of_crashing`, `test/commitmentblock_tests.cpp:commitments_readable_when_bodies_are_not`) that need "index says held, bytes genuinely unreadable" to keep working -- confirmed directly by re-running the full suite after the fix (3 fatal failures) and again after reverting it (clean). `WITHHOLD_COUNT=1` avoids the collision entirely (the harness's own count reaches 0 by the time the read-back happens) without touching production or harness code -- recorded here as a scope note, not fixed, since both sides of the interaction are `ArgsManager::DEBUG_ONLY` test-only flags with no production-facing impact. **10. Full suite.** 703 cases green (`!miner_tests`, verified on a genuine clean rebuild -- `make -C src clean` then a full rebuild -- after this task's own rapid edit/rebuild/revert churn, matching this session's own established caution about stale-binary false results); `miner_tests` unchanged (the one known pre-existing `CreateNewBlock_validity` failure). All three body-range/body-refetch functional tests (`feature_body_refetch.py`, `feature_body_refetch_backoff.py`, `feature_bodyrange_e2e.py`) pass against the same clean build | `source` (`net_processing.cpp`/`net.h`/`net.cpp`/`validation.cpp`/`bodyrange.h`/`bodyrange.cpp` read in full for the existing serving/upload-target/harness machinery before writing anything; F-143/F-150/F-152/F-153/F-159 re-read in full to confirm the naming-drift and the genuinely-open gap, not taken on this task's own brief alone); TDD (13 new `test/bodyrange_tests.cpp` cases, genuine RED against a wrong stub confirmed before GREEN); a real two-node functional test (`feature_bodyrange_e2e.py`) exercising the actual wire messages between two independent `raptoreumd` processes, not a mock peer; the harness-interaction bug independently reproduced twice (crash with the collision, clean after the workaround) and the attempted production fix's own regression independently reproduced too (3 fatal test failures) before reverting it; full suite run clean on a genuine clean rebuild before and after | **built 2026-09-29 -- the genuinely open per-connection serving budget is closed, F-159's end-to-end test gap is closed, and the "2.2.3b" naming drift is corrected; the harness-composition bug (item 9) is recorded, not fixed, as DEBUG_ONLY-only with no production impact** |
| F-219 | **2.4a audited: the row's own "multi-source body fetch" goal turns out to be already built, as an accidental consequence of 2.2.4-2.2's own single-source design (F-155-F-159/F-218) -- not a redesign, a real 4-node functional test proving a source that never answers (disconnected, "erased") does not permanently deny the fetcher its own history, since a second, independently-known source completes it. One real, previously-undiscovered production bug found while building the test -- recorded, not fixed, out of scope for this row.** | **1. What the row's brief asked to verify, verified by direct reading before writing any test.** `net_processing.cpp`'s `mapBodyRangeInFlight` (`net_processing.cpp:BodyRangeInFlight`) is genuinely single-source (at most one request per block hash, across all peers, at a time) exactly as the row's brief states, and its own doc comment cites C1 (multi-source scoring/selection) as a later phase -- confirmed, not assumed. But the brief's own framing ("is there already a slow, accidental form of try-someone-else") undersells what's actually there: the in-flight slot is freed at THREE independent points, and all three were checked against source, not inferred. (a) `net_processing.cpp`'s `BODYRANGE` response handler calls `EraseBodyRangeInFlight` the INSTANT `ValidateBodyRangeResponse` (shape only) passes -- BEFORE `ValidateBodyRangeChunkHashes` (content) ever runs, so a WRONG/corrupted response frees the slot before the peer is even misbehaved, not after. (b) `PeerLogicValidation::FinalizeNode` sweeps `mapBodyRangeInFlight` AND `g_body_retry_state` for every entry naming a disconnecting peer (F-155/F-157) -- confirmed this fires for ANY disconnect reason, not just a graceful one: `Misbehaving`'s own ban-threshold check (`net_processing.cpp:Misbehaving`, `state->fShouldBan`) is consulted by `PeerLogicValidation::SendMessages` (`net_processing.cpp:5325`) on that SAME peer's own next `SendMessages` pass, which then calls `connman->DisconnectNode`, which reaches `FinalizeNode` via the ordinary disconnect path -- so a WRONG (hash-mismatch, banned) peer is doubly covered: the slot frees immediately via (a), and the peer itself is later removed via this path regardless. (c) `IsBodyRangeRequestStale` (`bodyrange.h:IsBodyRangeRequestStale`) reaps a slot held by a peer that stays CONNECTED but never answers at all (SILENT), on a `BODY_RETRY_MAX_MICROS` (30s) ceiling, independent of (a)/(b) (F-156). **Conclusion, stated plainly**: for BOTH failure shapes the row's brief names -- WRONG (corrupted/hash-mismatched) and SILENT (never answers) -- the single-source slot already becomes available to a DIFFERENT peer promptly (immediately for WRONG, within 30s for SILENT, or immediately via an explicit disconnect for either), and nothing about single-source addressing prevents a DIFFERENT peer's own `SendMessages` pass from picking the block back up, since `vBodyBlocks` (`net_processing.cpp:FindNextBlocksToDownload`) is populated independently per peer from that peer's OWN `CAnnouncerRing::WasAnnounced` verdict -- no shared "who tried this already" state blocks a fresh peer. **2. `CAnnouncerRing`'s own API, checked against the brief's specific question ("can it answer which peers announced this hash, plural").** `announcerring.h`'s `CAnnouncerRing` is one-per-`CNodeState` (`net_processing.cpp:announcerRing`), storing only `hash -> height` for THAT peer -- there is no cross-peer "list every announcer of X" query, and none is needed: since every CONNECTED peer's own `SendMessages` call independently evaluates `WasAnnounced` for its OWN ring against every candidate block on every pass (`net_processing.cpp:6374`, `SendMessages`'s own "Message: getbodyrange" section), the peer SET itself already acts as the multi-candidate query -- iterating connected peers via their own periodic `SendMessages` calls IS the "ask every known candidate" mechanism, just decentralized rather than a single lookup. Extending the ring's own storage to answer "which peers" would duplicate state `SendMessages`'s own existing per-peer loop already provides for free. **No code change followed from this audit** -- confirmed by direct, cited reading that the failure-triggers-retry-with-a-different-peer property the row's brief describes as needing to be built was already a side effect of F-155-F-159/F-218's own crash-ordering and resource-leak discipline, not a gap. **3. The real test, `test/functional/feature_bodyrange_multisource_failover.py`.** Four real node processes (not two, not three -- see point 4): a fetcher (withholds one real block, same shape as `feature_bodyrange_e2e.py`), serverA (`-commitmentblocks=1`, deliberately WITHOUT `-servebodyrange` -- silent by design, matching the SILENT failure shape (c) above), a plain bridge node, and serverB (`-commitmentblocks=1 -servebodyrange=1`, the second candidate). The fetcher connects to serverA alone first; serverA's own silence is confirmed via `wait_for_debug_log` (a new test-local helper -- `assert_debug_log` only checks its expected lines when its own `with` block EXITS, so a caller needing to ACT on a message having arrived, here disconnecting serverA next, needs a real poll, not a check-at-exit); serverA is then explicitly disconnected (`disconnect_nodes`, exercising `FinalizeNode`'s own cleanup, point 1(b) above) and the fetcher is connected to serverB, whose own real GETBODYRANGE/BODYRANGE round trip (confirmed via `assert_debug_log` on both sides, matching `feature_bodyrange_e2e.py`'s own established pattern) recovers the body serverA never served. Confirmed 4/4 clean runs, non-flaky. **This is a confirmation test, not a RED-then-GREEN bug-fix test** -- point 1's own citations establish the mechanism already worked before this test was written; the test exists because the row's own brief explicitly asks for a real test proving it ("simulate one peer failing/getting banned... confirm a second peer then successfully serves it"), matching this project's own established convention (F-151's precedent: "an untested documented branch closed with a one-line test", not a fabricated RED). One genuine test-authoring bug was found and fixed while writing it, not production code: the first draft's `assert_debug_log` window opened AFTER `connect_nodes` had already returned, and on regtest with the fetcher exactly one block behind, the whole GETBODYRANGE/BODYRANGE round trip can complete within the SAME millisecond `connect_nodes`' own version-handshake wait returns in -- the window missed an event that had already happened. Fixed by moving `connect_nodes` itself inside the `assert_debug_log` block. **4. A real, previously-undiscovered production bug found while building this test -- recorded, not fixed, out of scope for this row.** The obvious three-node design (fetcher, serverA, serverB, with serverB syncing directly from serverA) reliably crashes serverA: `SIGABRT`, no assertion text, no debug symbols in the trace, immediately after serverA's own VERSION-handler sends `SENDCOMMITMENTS` to serverB (`net_processing.cpp:3586`) the instant TWO `NODE_COMMITMENTS`-advertising nodes (`-commitmentblocks` on BOTH sides) connect to each other. Reproduced twice, isolated specifically to this pairing (fetcher<->serverA, one side only, is the ALREADY-proven-safe shape every existing test in this session's F-155-218 corpus uses; confirmed by grep that no prior test ever connects two `-commitmentblocks` nodes to each other). Diagnosing a symbol-less SIGABRT in the commitments-negotiation path is a real, separate task from this row's own scope (body-fetch fault tolerance); worked around here with a fourth, unflagged relay node (serverA<->bridge<->serverB, both legs the proven-safe "one side has the bit" shape) rather than fixed. Flagged explicitly for whoever next touches commitment negotiation between two capable peers -- this exact reproduction (two `-commitmentblocks=1` nodes, `connect_nodes` between them) is the fastest way to hit it again. **5. Full suite and functional tests.** No production `src/*.cpp`/`.h` changed by this finding -- `docs/findings.md`/`docs/build-plan.md` and the two new test files (`test/functional/feature_bodyrange_multisource_failover.py`, registered in `test_runner.py` per F-158's own registration lesson) are the only additions. C++ suite unaffected (no new C++ test needed -- the property under test is inherently cross-process/wire-level, matching `feature_bodyrange_e2e.py`'s own established split of "glue exercised functionally, pure logic exercised at the unit level" for this exact subsystem); see F-220 for the C++ suite's own final count, since 2.4b's build ran in the same session. `feature_bodyrange_multisource_failover.py` confirmed 4/4 clean, `feature_bodyrange_e2e.py` re-confirmed still passing (no regression from this audit) | `source` (`net_processing.cpp`'s `BODYRANGE`/`GETBODYRANGE`/`FinalizeNode`/`SendMessages`/`Misbehaving` sections, `bodyrange.h`/`.cpp`, `announcerring.h` read in full before concluding anything was or wasn't already built; F-155-F-159/F-218 re-read in full, not taken on this row's own brief alone); a real 4-node functional test exercising actual wire messages and an actual disconnect between independent `raptoreumd` processes, confirmed 4/4 clean, non-flaky; the commitments-negotiation crash independently reproduced twice before being routed around rather than assumed transient | **audited 2026-09-29 -- the row's own multi-source fault-tolerance goal was already met by 2.2.4-2.2's own design, confirmed by a real test rather than left as a citation; no source change was needed or made. One real, unrelated production bug (a SIGABRT when two NODE_COMMITMENTS-capable nodes connect to each other) found and recorded, not fixed -- out of scope for this row** |
| F-220 | **2.4b built: verification of the body store's own bytes at rest against the block's own committed identifiers -- an operator-invokable RPC (`verifybodystore`, matching `getbodyrangecoverageheights`'s own F-212 precedent for exposing an internal consistency check) plus an automatic check wired into the EXISTING `CVerifyDB`/`-checkblocks`/`-checklevel` startup mechanism, deliberately fail-closed rather than live-repairing.** | **1. What "at rest" needed to mean, decided from source before writing anything.** `bodystore.h`'s own body store (`bdy*.dat`) is a REDUNDANT, independently-readable copy of a block's non-coinbase transactions, populated by 2.1.4/2.2.4's write paths -- but `validation.h:ReadCommitmentBlockFromDisk`'s own doc comment (confirmed still true by reading `validation.cpp:ReadCommitmentBlockFromDisk`) says today's tree still projects a block's commitment form from the WHOLE stored block in `blk*.dat`, not from any genuinely commitment-only storage (F-110's own still-current storage format) -- so nothing before this finding ever independently checked whether the SEPARATE `bdy*.dat` copy 2.2's own fetch/serve path actually relies on still agrees with it. `VerifyBodyRecordAtRest` (`validation.h`/`.cpp`) closes exactly that gap: read `pindex`'s own commitments via the existing `ReadCommitmentBlockFromDisk` (the source-of-truth identifier list), read the body-store's own record via the existing `bodystore.h:ReadBodyRecord`, and compare via the EXISTING `bodyrange.h:ValidateBodyRangeChunkHashes` (the SAME per-tx hash check 2.2.4's own fetch client already applies to a chunk arriving over the wire, F-158, applied here to the whole record read back from local disk instead of a network response) -- no new hash-comparison logic invented. Only meaningful for a block genuinely claimed complete (`BLOCK_HAVE_BODIES`, not merely `BLOCK_HAVE_BODY_RECORD` -- a withheld block has nothing claimed complete, reported `NOT_HELD`, never a failure of its own, matching `LookupServeableBodyPositionByHash`'s own F-144/F-145 "withheld reads as absent" convention). Five outcomes (`validation.h:BodyRecordVerification`): `OK`, `NOT_HELD`, `COMMITMENTS_UNREADABLE`, `BODY_UNREADABLE`, `COUNT_MISMATCH`, `HASH_MISMATCH`. Read-only: `cs_main` (a `RecursiveMutex`, `validation.cpp:107`) is taken only to snapshot `pindex`'s status bits and body position, never across the I/O that follows -- this project's own established discipline, matching every other body-related function audited for this row (F-149's own "no I/O under `cs_main`" precedent). **2. WHEN to run it, decided and argued, not defaulted.** Two triggers, each matching its own best precedent rather than one invented uniform rule. (a) **Manual, operator-invoked**: `verifybodystore(start_height, end_height)` (`rpc/net.cpp:verifybodystore`), matching `getbodyrangecoverageheights`'s own F-212 precedent exactly -- bounded by required params (never unbounded work), logs any mismatch found via `LogPrintf` (2.4's own stated "at minimum: log it clearly" requirement) in addition to returning it in the JSON result, so an unattended periodic caller doesn't depend on reading the return value. Read-only, never mutates `pindex`/`BLOCK_HAVE_BODIES`/the body-store index -- see point 3 for why. (b) **Automatic, at startup**: wired into the EXISTING `CVerifyDB::VerifyDB` (`validation.cpp:5887`) walk, immediately after its own pre-existing `!HaveBodies(pindex)` early-stop guard (which already confirms bodies ARE held for the pindex being checked) and unconditionally at every `nCheckLevel`, not gated behind a new tier -- this REUSES `-checkblocks`/`-checklevel` (`validation.h:DEFAULT_CHECKBLOCKS`/`DEFAULT_CHECKLEVEL`), this codebase's own ALREADY-established, ALREADY-configurable, ALREADY-runs-automatically-every-startup mechanism for exactly this class of check ("verify chain data is genuinely readable/correct"), rather than inventing a parallel opt-in flag. `-checkblocks=0` already means "all" (`init.cpp:848`'s own help text) -- an operator who just finished a bulk datadir copy for a new smartnode gets full-history body-store verification for free by passing the EXISTING flag, no new one needed. This directly answers the row's own "new-smartnode onboarding" framing (the natural trigger is exactly a fresh datadir an operator wants checked before trusting it) without the disproportionate cost of a mandatory full rehash on every ordinary restart (`DEFAULT_CHECKBLOCKS`=50 keeps the DEFAULT scope small, matching every other `VerifyDB` check's own existing cost profile). **3. What happens on a mismatch -- argued for fail-closed over live-repair, not assumed.** The RPC path: reports and logs, never mutates. The startup path: `VerifyDB` `return error(...)` on any non-OK/non-NOT_HELD result, exactly like every other corruption class it already detects -- this fails the WHOLE `VerifyDB` call, which `init.cpp:2593-2600` already turns into "Corrupted block database detected" / "Do you want to rebuild the block database now?" (confirmed by reading `init.cpp` directly, not assumed), prompting `-reindex` -- which ALREADY correctly rebuilds `bdy*.dat` from `blk*.dat` by reprocessing (2.1.3's own established `-reindex` decision, confirmed still true by reading `bodystore.h`'s own comment on it). **Live repair (clearing `BLOCK_HAVE_BODIES`/re-marking the body-store index unserveable on a RUNNING node) was considered and rejected, not merely not attempted**: grepped every `HaveBodies(` call site across the tree (`net_processing.cpp` x7, `validation.cpp` x8, `rest.cpp` x1) and found `validation.cpp:ConnectTip`'s own `!HaveBodies(pindexNew)` guard, `FindMostWorkChain`'s candidate-selection walk, and both `net_processing.cpp` serving sites all treat a CONNECTED block's `BLOCK_HAVE_BODIES` as a durable, one-way fact once true -- there is no existing precedent anywhere in this tree for un-asserting it on a live node (`PruneOneBlockFile` deliberately doesn't either yet, F-135's own still-open item), and `VerifyDB` itself only ever runs at startup, before `ActivateBestChain` or any network thread exists, where failing the whole load is both simpler and strictly safer than any live-mutation alternative that would need to reason about `cs_main`-guarded readers this check has no way to audit exhaustively. Recorded as an explicit, argued decision, not a default. **A known, honest limitation, stated not hidden**: even where a live-repair WERE attempted, the existing GETBODYRANGE fetch client is tip-path-only (F-154's own documented limitation) -- a deep-history mismatch could not be automatically re-fetched today regardless of this finding's own choice, which is exactly why fail-closed-at-startup (prompting a full `-reindex`, which has no such depth limit) is the stronger remedy for the row's own stated goal ("cannot propagate its hole to every node that joins later") for historical corruption specifically, not merely the simpler one. **4. TDD, genuine RED.** Six new `acceptancebit_tests.cpp` cases (`body_record_at_rest_verifies_ok_for_a_genuinely_intact_record`, `_reports_not_held_for_a_withheld_block`, `_detects_a_hash_mismatch_from_swapped_bodies`, `_detects_a_count_mismatch`, `_detects_an_unreadable_record`, `verifydb_fails_closed_on_a_corrupt_body_store_record`) -- `VerifyBodyRecordAtRest` did not exist before this change, so every one of them is genuine RED-via-non-existence before GREEN (matching F-192's own established precedent for this class of new-function TDD). Corruption is induced via the STORE'S OWN write API, not raw byte surgery, specifically for determinism: the hash-mismatch case swaps two real, already-accepted transactions' positions via `bodystore.h:WriteBodyRecord` at the SAME `FlatFilePos` (identical total size, so the record's own self-delimiting framing is untouched -- the shape a real bit-flip deep inside a transaction's own fields would also take: readable, self-consistent, wrong); the count-mismatch case drops the last body the same way; the unreadable case truncates the underlying body FILE at the record's own start (`fs::resize_file`, matching `bodystore_tests.cpp:read_body_at_does_not_depend_on_earlier_bodies_being_valid`'s own established `OpenBodyFile`-based corruption precedent) rather than depending on whatever random garbage bytes happen to parse as. `verifydb_fails_closed_on_a_corrupt_body_store_record` mines the corrupted block as the new CHAIN TIP specifically (rather than corrupting an existing `TestChain100Setup` ancestor) so truncating its own record touches nothing else -- it is the last thing written to its body file. **5. Mutation-tested.** Three mutations against `VerifyBodyRecordAtRest`'s own three branch conditions, each independently applied/reverted against a real incremental rebuild, all killed: the `bodies.size() != commitments.vCommitments.size()` count check inverted -- killed by 3 tests (`verifies_ok`, `detects_a_count_mismatch`, `detects_a_hash_mismatch...`, each independently). The `!ValidateBodyRangeChunkHashes(...)` result negated -- killed by 2 tests. The `NOT_HELD` gate disabled entirely (`if (false)`) -- killed by 1 test, and confirms directly (not merely argued) that a withheld block's underlying body-store bytes are ALREADY genuinely correct underneath the deferred status bit (F-134's own "`SaveBodyToDisk` runs unconditionally" design) -- `VerifyBodyRecordAtRest` would report `OK` for a withheld block if this gate were ever removed, which is exactly why the gate, not the underlying data, is what makes `NOT_HELD` the honest answer. All three mutations independently reverted and confirmed clean (`git diff` empty) before the final rebuild. **6. Full suite.** 709 cases (`!miner_tests`, up from F-218's 703 -- the six new cases above), confirmed green on a genuine `make -C src clean` + full rebuild (this session's own F-144/F-218-established caution about incremental-rebuild artifacts, warranted here too after several rapid mutation edit/rebuild/revert cycles); `miner_tests` unchanged (the one known pre-existing `CreateNewBlock_validity` failure). `verifybodystore` smoke-tested end to end against a real running `raptoreumd`/`raptoreum-cli` (help text renders, a real call against a freshly-mined regtest chain returns `{"checked":1,"ok":1,"not_held":0,"mismatches":[]}`) -- not just unit-tested in isolation | `source` (`validation.h`/`.cpp`, `bodystore.h`/`.cpp`, `bodyrange.h`, `chain.h`'s `BLOCK_HAVE_BODIES`/`BLOCK_HAVE_BODY_RECORD`/`GetBodyPos` read in full before designing anything; every `HaveBodies(` call site across the tree grepped and read before deciding live-repair was unsafe, not assumed; `init.cpp`'s own `VerifyDB`-failure-to-error-message path read directly); TDD (6 new tests, genuine RED via non-existence); mutation-tested (3/3 killed, one of which independently confirmed a design fact -- withheld bodies are already correct underneath -- rather than merely a code path); a real end-to-end RPC smoke test against a live node, not only `boost::test`; full suite run clean on a genuine clean rebuild | **built 2026-09-29 -- store-at-rest verification is now real, exposed via RPC and wired into the existing startup verification path; live repair was considered and explicitly rejected in favour of fail-closed, citing the specific `HaveBodies()` call sites that make it unsafe** |
| F-221 | **F-219's SIGABRT, real-diagnosed: `SENDCOMMITMENTS`'s wire string overflows `COMMAND_SIZE`, aborting BOTH peers the instant two `NODE_COMMITMENTS`-capable nodes ever exchange it -- not lock ordering, not reentrancy, not an uncaught exception, a plain string-length bug F-219's non-debug build had no symbols to name. Fixed; isolated to this one message (checked, not assumed).** Rebuilt `raptoreumd` with real debug info (`./configure --enable-debug`, confirmed `-O0 -g3 -DDEBUG_CORE -DDEBUG_LOCKORDER` active in `src/Makefile:2885`) before reproducing anything, per this session's own standing lesson against theorising from an unpatched/unsymbolised build. Two real `raptoreumd` processes, both `-commitmentblocks=1`, connected directly via `addnode` (regtest) -- reproduced immediately, deterministically, on BOTH sides: `raptoreumd: protocol.cpp:177: CMessageHeader::CMessageHeader(const unsigned char (&)[4], const char*, unsigned int): Assertion \`pszCommand[i] == 0' failed.` **Root cause**: `NetMsgType::SENDCOMMITMENTS = "sendcommitments"` (`protocol.cpp:SENDCOMMITMENTS`) is 15 bytes; `CMessageHeader::COMMAND_SIZE` (`protocol.h:COMMAND_SIZE`) is 12. `CMessageHeader`'s own constructor (`protocol.cpp:CMessageHeader::CMessageHeader`) copies at most `COMMAND_SIZE` bytes from `pszCommand` then asserts the source string was already null-terminated by that point (line 177) -- always false for this string, so constructing this message's header aborts unconditionally, every time, on whichever side attempts it. The full call chain, traced end to end, not assumed: the VERACK handler's `connman->PushMessage(pfrom, msgMaker.Make(NetMsgType::SENDCOMMITMENTS))` (`net_processing.cpp:3586`) builds a `CSerializedNetMsg` carrying the raw command string (`CNetMsgMaker::Make`, `netmessagemaker.h`, does no length check) and hands it to `CConnman::PushMessage` (`net.cpp:CConnman::PushMessage`), which is where `CMessageHeader hdr(Params().MessageStart(), msg.command.c_str(), nMessageSize)` (`net.cpp:3676`) actually constructs the header and hits the assert -- synchronously, on the same message-processing call stack that received VERACK, confirmed by the debug.log line ("sending sendcommit (0 bytes) peer=0") that `PushMessage`'s own `LogPrint` (`net.cpp:3669`) emits immediately before constructing the header. **Why this needed BOTH sides, and why it's not reentrancy**: `ShouldNegotiateCommitments` (`net_processing.cpp:ShouldNegotiateCommitments`) gates the send on both the local AND the remote service bits claiming `NODE_COMMITMENTS` -- so every single-sided pairing in the existing F-155-220 corpus never once called `CNetMsgMaker::Make(NetMsgType::SENDCOMMITMENTS)` at all, and the bug simply went unexercised, not unexercised-by-luck under contention. Confirmed both nodes crash independently and identically (each constructs and sends its own copy of the same oversized message to the other), not from any interaction between them. **Ruled out, not assumed**: an uncaught exception escaping the handler -- `ProcessMessages`' own `try`/`catch(...)` (`net_processing.cpp:5438-5470`) wraps every `ProcessMessage` call, including this one, with a catch-all that would have caught and logged (`PrintExceptionContinue`) any C++ exception; `assert()` calls `abort()` directly via `SIGABRT`, entirely bypassing exception unwinding, so no catch block anywhere in the stack could have intercepted it -- this was never an exception. **Fix**: shortened the wire string to `"sendcommit"` (10 bytes) -- only the string literal changes; the `NetMsgType::SENDCOMMITMENTS` C++ symbol and every call site referencing it are untouched. Matches this project's own established convention: `SENDDSQUEUE`'s wire string is already abbreviated to `"senddsq"` (7 bytes, not `"senddsqueue"`) for the identical reason. **Sibling sweep (checked, not assumed)**: all 65 `NetMsgType` string literals in `protocol.cpp` measured against `COMMAND_SIZE` -- `SENDCOMMITMENTS` was the only violator; `SENDCMPCT` (9), `SENDHEADERS` (11), `SENDDSQUEUE`'s wire string (7), `QWATCH` (6), and every other message type are all comfortably within the 12-byte limit. Not a broader pattern -- isolated to this one identifier. **Regression test**: new `src/test/protocol_tests.cpp` (registered in `Makefile.test.include`), a general invariant test -- every entry in `getAllNetMessageTypes()` must fit `COMMAND_SIZE` -- checking the same length the real constructor enforces without calling that constructor (calling it with an oversized command would `abort()` the whole test binary, not fail a `BOOST_CHECK`). Confirmed genuine RED against the pre-fix source (single failure, exactly the `SENDCOMMITMENTS` overflow, no other message implicated) and GREEN after the fix. Guards every future message type, not just this one. **Live re-verification, post-fix**: the same two-process direct-pairing repro, rebuilt -- both nodes stay up; `debug.log` on both sides shows `sending sendcommit (0 bytes) peer=0` / `received: sendcommit (0 bytes) peer=0`; clean RPC `stop` on both. `feature_bodyrange_multisource_failover.py` (F-219's own workaround test) re-run against the fixed binary and still passes end to end, confirming no regression to the existing single-sided-pairing coverage. **Full C++ suite**: 719 cases, 1 failure (`miner_tests/CreateNewBlock_validity`, the same pre-existing failure every prior row in this doc names, not a regression); `protocol_tests` itself green in the same run. | `source` (a real `--enable-debug` build reproducing the crash twice with two live `raptoreumd` processes before any fix was written; `protocol.cpp`/`protocol.h`/`net.cpp`/`net_processing.cpp`/`netmessagemaker.h` read in full for the call chain; all 65 `NetMsgType` strings measured directly, not sampled; `src/test/protocol_tests.cpp` RED-then-GREEN; `feature_bodyrange_multisource_failover.py` re-confirmed passing) | **fixed 2026-09-29 (F-221) -- SENDCOMMITMENTS's wire string shortened to fit COMMAND_SIZE, general regression test added, confirmed isolated to this one message, live-reproduced both broken and fixed with real processes** |
| F-222 | **3.6 closed: the mainnet `defaultAssumeValid` typo is real and current -- confirmed live on `master` AND on `upstream/develop` (its actively-maintained tip, 2026-09-17, 11 days before this audit) -- and both it and `nMinimumChainWork` were also badly stale (anchored to block 421457, Oct 2022). Fixed the typo and moved both anchors forward together to a live-verified block 1,340,136 (100,000 blocks / ~1.0% chainwork behind the real tip, vs. the old anchor's 1,018,679 blocks / ~73% behind); the checkpoint map's own staleness is measured but deliberately left open for the owner, not fixed here.** **1. The typo, proven, not inferred.** `uint256::SetHex` (`uint256.cpp:SetHex`) only strips a leading `0x`/`0X` pair via `psz[0]=='0' && ToLower(psz[1])=='x'`; the mainnet `defaultAssumeValid` literal began with `ox` (letter o), which fails that check, so parsing falls through to the hex-digit scan starting at the `o` itself -- `HexDigit('o') == -1` on the very first call, the scan loop body never executes, and `m_data` is left at its post-`memset` all-zero state. Constructed directly, not just read: `src/test/uint256_tests.cpp`'s `setHex_letter_o_prefix_silently_parses_to_zero` builds `uint256S("ox6fb0...")` against the real pre-fix literal and asserts `.IsNull()` -- true. **Confirmed live on real, currently-maintained upstream, not just this playground's own history**: `git show master:src/chainparams.cpp` has the identical `ox...` literal, and so does `git show upstream/develop:src/chainparams.cpp` at its current tip (`d02e9132`, 2026-09-17), the actively-developed branch F-217's own open PRs (#480/#481) target. Git-blamed to `6806d2da38` ("clean up coding style," PR #297, 2023-06-24) -- a pure reformat; the parent commit (`bcc6c2730`, PR #267, 2023-02-17) already had the typo, so it was introduced then, when the anchor was bumped to block 421457, and has sat live and unnoticed in default mainnet builds for 3.5+ years. **2. The bug's real runtime effect, traced.** `defaultAssumeValid.IsNull()==true` means `init.cpp`'s own `hashAssumeValid` (`init.cpp:hashAssumeValid`) also ends up null (`GetHex()` of the zero hash round-trips through `uint256S` to zero again), so `ConnectBlock`'s own `if (!hashAssumeValid.IsNull())` guard (`validation.cpp:ConnectBlock`) never opens -- the entire signature-verification-skip optimisation has been permanently, silently dead on every mainnet node's default configuration since PR #267: every IBD re-verifies every historical block's scripts from genesis, no error or warning that the setting failed to take. **3. `nMinimumChainWork` staleness, quantified against mario's real, fully-synced mainnet node** (`raptoreum-cli -conf=~/.raptoreumcore/raptoreum.conf`, read-only). Old anchor: block 421457, chainwork `0x0eead474ccbc59` (matches the hardcoded constant exactly -- this constant was never typo'd, only stale), real timestamp 2022-10-16 15:47:15 UTC. Real tip at audit time (2026-09-29): height 1,440,136, chainwork `0x19ccf75953ec0a`. Gap: **1,018,679 blocks, ~73.0% more real cumulative chainwork at the tip than the hardcoded floor** -- confirmed by two independent RPC calls (`getblockchaininfo`, `getblock <tip> 1`). Not decorative: `IsInitialBlockDownload` (`validation.cpp:IsInitialBlockDownload`), `ConnectBlock`'s assumevalid gate (`validation.cpp:ConnectBlock`), and `AcceptBlock`'s low-work-chain DoS rejection (`validation.cpp:AcceptBlock`) all read it live -- a stale floor genuinely weakens the eclipse/fake-low-work-chain resistance the row's own text names as the point of 3.6, today, not just historically. **4. Typo-only-fix vs. move-the-anchor-forward: argued choice, not the reflexive smaller fix.** Chose to move both constants forward together, not just correct the `0x`. Reasoning: (a) both are **floors**, not release-pinned facts like a checkpoint -- nothing in this codebase ties them to a release cadence the way `checkpoints.h` does for checkpoints (point 6); (b) a wrong-but-real, deeply-buried historical block carries no correctness risk -- `nMinimumChainWork` only needs to be *at or below* the true chain's cumulative work at that height, permanently fixed once the block is old enough, and `defaultAssumeValid` fails open (silently inert, exactly today's bug) if the hash isn't an ancestor of the best header, never fails unsafe; (c) mario's node gives exactly the sourcing standard this fix needs. New anchor: **block 1,340,136** (tip-100,000 at audit time), chosen for two independent, cited reasons rather than a round number: **1,000x `COINBASE_MATURITY`** (`consensus/consensus.h:COINBASE_MATURITY`, this codebase's own stated 100-block finality margin) of burial depth, and because it lands at **~4.6 months old** relative to today, matching the *original* anchor's own real precedent -- block 421457 was itself ~4 months old (2022-10-16) when PR #267 shipped it (2023-02-17), per `git blame`/`git log` on the introducing commit. Hash and chainwork sourced from mario's node and cross-checked two ways (`getblock <hash> 1` and `getblockheader <hash>`, identical both times): hash `8ef694133cd8981a14f4042d256cdce823cb5686dadfcda5ad843b97090cfccf`, chainwork `0x198d0e6ffa12df`, real timestamp 2026-05-10 17:40:10 UTC, 100,001 confirmations at query time. New gap to tip: **100,000 blocks, ~1.0% chainwork** -- a floor that actually does the job 3.6's own text describes, versus the old anchor's 73%. **5. The checkpoint at 394273 -- audited, NOT a second typo, left alone.** It's missing the `0x` prefix the other three checkpoint entries have, which looks like the same bug class as point 1 -- it is not. `uint256::SetHex` never *requires* a `0x` prefix, it only strips one when present (point 1's own citation); the string starts with a valid hex digit (`'0'`), so it parses identically either way -- proven by `src/test/uint256_tests.cpp`'s `checkpoint_394273_hash_without_0x_prefix_parses_correctly`, which constructs both the with- and without-prefix forms and asserts equality. Verified against mario's node (`getblockhash 394273` / `getblock`): matches exactly. **6. Checkpoint staleness -- quantified, deliberately left open, not decided here.** Real gap: 394273 to the real tip (1,440,136) is **1,045,863 blocks with zero checkpoint coverage** -- deeper than the gap this fix closed for the other two constants. Not fixed in this pass. Reasoning, matching this project's own established restraint pattern (F-213/F-217's "concrete option, owner decides the number" shape): `checkpoints.h`'s own doc comment (`checkpoints.h:17-18`, "Block-chain checkpoints are compiled-in sanity checks. They are updated every release or three.") ties this specifically to a **release cadence** this research branch does not have and should not simulate -- unlike an assumevalid/minimumchainwork floor, a checkpoint is a **permanent, unconditional** "reject any fork not including this block" declaration (`validation.cpp:ContextualCheckBlockHeader`, `bad-fork-prior-to-checkpoint`), and picking that block is a project-release decision, not a technical one a research pass should make unilaterally. A concrete, live-verified candidate is recorded for the owner (in-source comment above `checkpointData` in `chainparams.cpp`): the same block chosen in point 4 (height 1,340,136, hash `8ef694...cfccf`) -- not added as a fifth entry pending explicit sign-off. **7. Tests, genuine RED before GREEN.** New `src/test/chainparams_anchors_tests.cpp` (6 cases, registered in `Makefile.test.include`) constructs `CreateChainParams(CBaseChainParams::MAIN)` directly (this codebase's own established pattern, `pow_tests.cpp`/`commitmentmode_registration_tests.cpp`) and checks: `defaultAssumeValid` is not null; it and `nMinimumChainWork` match the new anchor's real, live-sourced values exactly; the new `nMinimumChainWork` exceeds the old stale one; testnet/devnet/regtest's placeholder anchors are untouched; the checkpoint map still has exactly 4 entries topping out at 394273 with its real hash. **Genuine RED confirmed**: reverted `src/chainparams.cpp` alone (via a saved patch, not `git stash` -- the shared stash stack has other sessions on it), rebuilt, and 4 of 6 cases failed exactly as expected -- `defaultAssumeValid.IsNull()` true, hash/chainwork mismatches printed against the real pre-fix values, `nMinimumChainWork` failing its own "exceeds the old anchor" check (old == old is not `>`) -- then reapplied the patch, rebuilt (binary mtime checked fresh both times), reran: all 6 GREEN. Two further `uint256_tests.cpp` cases prove the underlying parsing mechanics directly (points 1, 5), independent of `chainparams.cpp`. **8. Full suite.** 727 cases (719 pre-existing + this fix's 8 new: 6 `chainparams_anchors_tests` + 2 `uint256_tests`), 1 failure -- `miner_tests/CreateNewBlock_validity` (`test/miner_tests.cpp:413`), the same pre-existing failure every prior row in this doc names, confirmed by line number and assertion text, not a regression; binary mtime checked fresh before trusting the run. | `source` (direct read of `uint256.cpp`/`uint256.h` `SetHex`, `validation.cpp`'s `IsInitialBlockDownload`/`ConnectBlock`/`AcceptBlock`/`ContextualCheckBlockHeader`, `checkpoints.h`/`checkpoints.cpp`, `consensus/consensus.h:COINBASE_MATURITY`, `init.cpp`'s `hashAssumeValid` wiring); live RPC against mario's real, fully-synced mainnet node (`getblockchaininfo`/`getblockhash`/`getblock`/`getblockheader`, cross-checked two ways for the new anchor); `git blame`/`git log`/`git show` on `chainparams.cpp` across this repo, `master`, and `upstream/develop`; TDD (genuine RED via a saved-patch revert + rebuild, not `git stash`, then GREEN); full suite run clean except the one known pre-existing `miner_tests/CreateNewBlock_validity` failure | **closed 2026-09-29 (F-222) -- 3.6's typo and both stale-anchor gaps fixed on mainnet only; the checkpoint gap is measured and flagged, a concrete candidate recorded in-source, final sign-off left to the owner, matching this project's own restraint precedent** |

| F-223 | **3.4 built, all three named bugs fixed, plus one more-severe bug found in the same area while fixing the second: intra-block asset-op visibility (B8), undo keyed by (assetId, tx index) (B9), and the dead distribution-type bound -- and a pre-existing disconnect-time existence-check bug that made B9's own fix unreachable via the real `DisconnectBlock` path until fixed too.** **1. Bug 1/B8 (intra-block visibility), one root cause for both of F-216's own findings.** `ProcessSpecialTxsInBlock` (`evo/specialtx.cpp:115`) validates every special tx in a block in ONE pass -- `CheckSpecialTx` for every tx, in order -- entirely BEFORE `ConnectBlock`'s own SEPARATE per-tx loop (`validation.cpp`, `UpdateCoins`/`AddAssets`, called after `ProcessSpecialTxsInBlock` returns) ever mutates `assetsCache`. So every asset tx in a block was checked against the identical PRE-block cache state, regardless of position -- confirmed directly by reading `ConnectBlock`'s own call order, not assumed. This is the SAME single root cause behind BOTH of F-216's "same-block" surprise candidates: the dup-name silent-drop (`CAssetsCache::InsertAsset`'s own duplicate guard, `assets/assets.cpp`, returns `false` on the second registration, unchecked by its caller, `assets.cpp:AddAssets`) and the mint-cap bypass (`checkAssetMintAmount`'s cap check, `evo/providertx.cpp`, reads pre-block `mintCount` for every mint in the block). **Fixed**: `ProcessSpecialTxsInBlock` now builds a scratch `CAssetsCache`, copy-constructed from `*assetsCache` once per call, and dispatches `CheckSpecialTx` against THAT for the three asset tx types; immediately after each one's own check succeeds, `AddAssets(*ptr_tx, pindex->nHeight, i, scratchAssetsCache.get())` applies its effect to the SAME scratch cache, so the next tx in the block sees it. Matches B8's own suggested fix verbatim ("Apply asset updates to a scratch cache inside the check loop", `docs/transaction-decoupling.md`). `assetsCache` itself -- the real, undo-recording cache `ConnectBlock`'s own later per-tx loop mutates -- is completely untouched by this change; the scratch copy is discarded at the end of the function, never flushed anywhere. **A real side effect, discovered live, not assumed**: `CMintAssetTx`/`CUpdateAssetTx::MakeSignString` (`evo/providertx.cpp:817`/`:799`) bake the asset's CURRENT `circulatingSupply` into the signed message -- a deliberate anti-conflict mechanism, matching `mintasset`'s own RPC-level `existsAssetTxConflict` guard's single-mint-in-flight intent. With this fix live, a second same-block mint signed against the (now stale) pre-block `circulatingSupply` fails signature verification rather than the cap check specifically -- confirmed against a real built node (`feature_characterise_assets.py`, `characterise_double_mint_exceeds_cap_in_block`): the 6-mints-against-cap-5 block is REJECTED, but the live reason is `bad-mint-assets-sig`, not `bad-max-mint-count` -- the cap is still not bypassed, which is what matters; a wallet that wants two dependent same-block mints to both connect now needs to sign the second against the expected post-first-mint state, exactly matching what a real sequential-mint wallet flow would already need to do. **2. Bug 2/B9 (undo keyed by tx index) plus a separate, more severe bug found while fixing it.** `CAssetsCache::UndoMintAsset`/`UndoUpdateAsset` (`assets/assets.cpp:248`/`:192`) matched their undo record by `assetId` alone against the block's own `vUndoData` vector, with NO `break` -- so the loop always ended on whichever matching record was LAST in the vector, regardless of which tx was actually being undone. Real reproduction built and run (not reasoned abstractly): two mints on one asset in one block (mintCount 1->3), block invalidated -- pre-fix, disconnecting the block left `mintCount=2` (the state AFTER the first mint, i.e. as if only the SECOND mint had been undone), not the correct `mintCount=1` (the true pre-block-2 state); the earlier tx's own undo record was never actually selected, only ever the later one's, coincidentally correct only for the LAST tx disconnected in the reverse loop. **Fixed**: `CBlockAssetUndo` (`assets/assetsdb.h`) gained a `txIndex` field (widened, not a new consensus commitment -- this is local, node-regenerable-by-reindex undo data, never hashed into anything, so no activation gate is needed); `UndoMintAsset`/`UndoUpdateAsset` now take a `txIndex` parameter and match `item.first == assetId && item.second.txIndex == txIndex`, with a `break`; threaded from `AddAssets` (recording, gains an `nTxIndex` parameter) through `UpdateCoins` (`validation.cpp`) and `ConnectBlock`'s own `i` loop variable at the write side, and from `DisconnectBlock`'s own reverse-order `i` loop variable at the read side. **A separate, more severe bug found empirically while building the regression test for B9, not assumed or theorised**: the new test's own `InvalidateBlock` call FAILED OUTRIGHT (returned `false`, no `CValidationState` rejection reason at all -- inconsistent with a validation rejection, consistent with a bare `DISCONNECT_FAILED`) before the txIndex fix was even reachable. Root cause: both `UndoMintAsset` and `UndoUpdateAsset` had their own outer existence guard, `if (mapAsset.count(assetTx.assetId) > 0)`, which checks ONLY that ONE `CAssetsCache` INSTANCE's own local map -- but `DisconnectTip` (`validation.cpp:3280`) and `ConnectTip` (`validation.cpp:3447`) each construct a FRESH, EMPTY `CAssetsCache assetCache;` on EVERY SINGLE call, so this guard was `false` for every asset created in an earlier block, i.e. always, for every disconnect this code had ever been asked to perform outside a same-call same-block mint/create combination. `DisconnectBlock` treats a `false` return from either function as `DISCONNECT_FAILED`; `ActivateBestChainStep`'s own caller answers a failed disconnect with `AbortNode` (F-29, already in this document) -- so a real reorg disconnecting ANY block containing an UPDATE_ASSET or MINT_ASSET transaction would have aborted the node. Asymmetric with `TRANSACTION_NEW_ASSET`'s own disconnect branch (`validation.cpp`), which correctly uses `CheckIfAssetExists` (has its own DB fallback) rather than a raw `mapAsset.count()`. **Fixed** by replacing the broken guard with a direct call to `GetAssetMetaData` (local map -> global `passetsCache` -> `passetsdb`, the correct three-tier fallback), which both functions already had to call next anyway -- not gating it, since `GetAssetMetaData` already returns `false` exactly when the asset truly cannot be found anywhere. Confirmed empirically: the exact same test's `InvalidateBlock` call went from `ok=0`/no-reason to `ok=1` the moment this guard was replaced, independent of the `txIndex` fix (verified as two separate observations in sequence, not merely inferred). Not something F-216 found (F-216 only exercised connect-time behaviour; disconnect was never reached) and not named by the row's own text, but directly in scope: it made the row's own literal fix instruction unreachable via the real code path. **Deslopped in the same area**: `DisconnectBlock`'s own `CAssetsCache tempCache(*assetsCache);` (`validation.cpp`, immediately before the reverse-order undo loop) was dead code -- declared, never read anywhere in the function -- that ALSO unconditionally dereferenced `assetsCache` regardless of nullability; removed (all three of `DisconnectBlock`'s real callers already pass a non-null pointer, so no behaviour changes). **3. Bug 3 (dead distribution-type bound), F-216's own diagnosis confirmed and fixed.** `CheckNewAssetTx`/`CheckUpdateAssetTx` (`evo/providertx.cpp:203`/`:274`) each had `if (assetTx.type < 0 && assetTx.type > 3)` -- `type` is `uint8_t` (`evo/providertx.h`), so `type < 0` is always false, and `&&` with an always-false operand makes the whole bound dead code; no `type` value was ever rejected by this specific check (F-216's own finding, confirmed by direct read here). Downstream masking confirmed too: `collateralAddress.IsNull() && type != 0` (the very next check) happens to reject most real-world misuse, but only incidentally, and only when `collateralAddress` is left null -- a deliberately non-null `collateralAddress` sails through both checks with an out-of-range `type`. **Fixed**: `type > 3` (drops the always-false half rather than flipping `&&` to `||`, since `type < 0` can never be true for this field's actual type -- equivalent, and does not depend on `type` staying unsigned forever the way an `||` fix would silently continue to). Confirmed no live consequence anywhere downstream that switches or indexes on `type` (grepped every `.type` read in `assets/`+`evo/providertx.cpp`; every consumer compares against a specific known value, never indexes or switches unboundedly) -- matches F-216's own characterisation exactly. **4. Why all three were worth fixing now, not just recording** (per this row's own "no longer fork-critical... still real divergences" framing, and the owner's explicit "decide, don't assume 'fix all three'" instruction): all three are genuine correctness bugs on the EXISTING, already-live RTM network, independent of decoupling; all three had precise, source-confirmed root causes and low-risk, narrowly-scoped fixes (a scratch cache local to one function; a widened, non-consensus undo record; a one-line dead-bound repair); F-216 had already built the reproduction harness for two of them; and the design doc's own security review (`docs/transaction-decoupling.md` SS14.3, B8/B9) explicitly argued they mattered enough to be "must ship before activation" under the OLD (pre-v8) design, where changing them after activation would itself have been a hard fork. **The "no longer fork-critical" framing precisely**: v8 deferred the asset-state commitment work entirely (`docs/transaction-decoupling.md` SS13.A, "deferred in v8, independently useful" -- replay-then-prune from the Smartnode tier covers what the state root would have, per SS7A.3/SS12) -- so there is no root committing asset state into any block header, and B8/B9 fixing (or not fixing) this bug can never itself become "a hard fork to change later," because nothing depends on `CAssetsCache`'s exact bit-for-bit content being canonical across nodes. That does NOT mean the divergence risk vanished: B9's bug, left unfixed, is a genuine, deterministically-reproducible corruption of a node's OWN local asset-metadata cache on any real reorg touching a multi-mint-per-asset block (and separately, per the existence-check bug above, an availability/crash risk via `AbortNode` on ANY reorg touching an update/mint-asset block at all) -- both real today, on live mainnet, entirely independent of this project. **5. TDD, genuine RED confirmed for all three before any fix, then GREEN.** New `src/test/assets_tests.cpp` cases, run against this worktree's own built binary: `assets_same_block_duplicate_name_rejected` and `assets_same_block_mint_exceeds_cap_rejected` (bug 1) both failed pre-fix (block ACCEPTED, height advanced) and pass post-fix (block REJECTED); `assets_mint_undo_keyed_by_tx_index` (bug 2) failed pre-fix in two DISTINCT ways in sequence as each layer was fixed (first `InvalidateBlock` failing outright on the existence-check bug, then -- once that was fixed -- `mintCount`/`circulatingSupply` landing on the wrong value from the txIndex bug) and passes post-fix (`mintCount=1`, `circulatingSupply=10`, matching block 1's own post-state exactly); `assets_distribution_type_out_of_range_rejected` (bug 3, `collateralAddress` deliberately set non-null to isolate the bound from the masking check) failed pre-fix and passes post-fix. All nine `assets_creation_tests` cases (four pre-existing plus the five new) pass together. **6. Live-reconfirmed against a real built node**, per this session's own standing caution not to trust the C++ suite alone: `test/functional/feature_characterise_assets.py`'s own two block-level "surprise candidate" rows (F-216's own harness) re-run against this worktree's real `raptoreumd`, converted from pure observation to real assertions now that the fix is confirmed -- both now show the block REJECTED (`bad-assets-dup-name` for the dup-name row; `bad-mint-assets-sig` for the cap row, per the `MakeSignString` side effect above); the `type=99`/dup-name issuance row now shows `bad-assets-distibution-type` instead of `ACCEPTED (no rejection)`, removed from `accepted_ok`. **One pre-existing, unrelated harness issue found while re-running, not introduced by this fix**: the file's own separate "mint: assetId does not exist" row fails with `HARNESS ERROR: max() arg is an empty sequence` (a `fresh_tx()` coin-selection exhaustion, the same class of issue that file's own docstring already documents finding empirically elsewhere) -- reproduced identically on two independent full runs, in a row this fix never touches and that executes before any UTXO consumption this fix could have changed;
